## Ordered content

PetSaathi Phase 7 — Society Partnership Phase 🏢🐾

Executive decision

Approve Phase 7, with one important framing:

Phase 7 is not mainly a marketing phase. It is a B2B2C operating-density phase.

PetSaathi will partner with an apartment society, RWA, AOA or residential facility manager, but the actual service relationship remains between:

PetSaathi

+ resident pet parent

+ authorised sitter

The society provides controlled access, local credibility, communication channels and demand concentration. It should not become responsible for individual bookings, pet-care decisions or customer payments unless a specific corporate contract says otherwise.

1. What Phase 7 changes

Before society partnerships

Customer in Area A

Sitter in Area B

Long travel time

Low booking density

Expensive customer acquisition

Irregular repeat demand

Difficult gate access

After a successful partnership

Several customers in one society

↓

Locally approved sitter pool

↓

Predictable service windows

↓

Lower travel time

↓

More daily bookings per sitter

↓

Same-sitter continuity

↓

Walking packs and recurring demand

The advantage is not simply that the society promotes PetSaathi. The real advantage is operational density.

2. Why society partnerships can become a moat

Higher trust

Residents may be more comfortable when:

The service has been reviewed by the society committee.

Sitter identity and training checks are visible.

Security has an approved-entry process.

A local support contact exists.

Other residents have already used the service.

However, use precise wording:

Society access approved

Identity verification reviewed

Pet-care training completed

Do not claim:

100% safe

Risk-free sitter

Society guarantees the service

Lower customer-acquisition cost

Instead of acquiring every customer separately, PetSaathi can reach residents through:

Society WhatsApp or communication apps

Lobby posters

Pet-parent meetings

Resident events

Management announcements

Existing customer referrals

The actual benefit must be measured:

Society CAC =

Total society acquisition and activation cost

÷

First-time paying residents

A partnership with many leads but few paid bookings is not automatically successful.

Better booking density

The strongest Phase 7 metric is:

Completed bookings

per active society

per week

Density can reduce:

Sitter travel time

Transport cost

Late arrivals

Idle gaps

Replacement time

Support complexity

It can also allow one sitter to complete several non-overlapping services in the same society without travelling across the city.

Better repeat demand

Society-based services are especially suitable for:

Daily dog walking

Five- and ten-walk packs

Weekly pet sitting

Festival care

Travel-period support

Same-sitter schedules

Controlled boarding referrals

Repeat behaviour should be measured through completed second services—not only package purchases.

Easier gate and access operations

Society partnerships can create a structured process for:

Sitter registration

Visitor approvals

ID verification display

Entry and exit records

Approved service hours

Pet-lift or common-area instructions

Emergency-entry escalation

Replacement-sitter approval

This reduces situations where a sitter is technically assigned but cannot enter the property.

3. Important legal and operational boundary

PetSaathi must keep commercial pet-care services separate from a society’s responsibilities relating to community animals.

The Animal Welfare Board of India’s July 2025 guidance addresses RWAs and AOAs directly and states that they have responsibilities under the Animal Birth Control Rules, including structured feeding arrangements and maintaining records relating to community animals. The guidance discusses designated feeding areas and times, vaccination/sterilisation records and coordination with local bodies.

Therefore, PetSaathi must not position its paid partnership as:

A method to remove or exclude community dogs

A replacement for an RWA’s statutory or community-animal duties

A system for harassing feeders or pet owners

A promise to make a society “animal-free”

A tool for imposing blanket pet restrictions

PetSaathi may offer a separate optional coordination module for community-animal records, vaccination drives or approved feeding-point coordination, but this should be contractually and operationally separate from paid dog walking, sitting and boarding.

AWBI has also repeatedly issued guidance involving RWAs, apartment associations and community-animal care, including the need to avoid harassment and to coordinate designated feeding arrangements.

4. Society partnership stakeholders

A partnership usually requires more than approval from one committee member.

Society stakeholders

### Table 1

| Stakeholder | Responsibility |
| --- | --- |
| RWA/AOA committee | Partnership approval and policy alignment |
| Facility manager | Daily operational coordination |
| Security supervisor | Sitter entry and replacement procedures |
| Residents | Individual service registration and payment |
| Pet-parent group | Feedback, demand validation and referrals |
| Non-pet residents | Safety, cleanliness and common-area concerns |
| Local veterinarian | Optional emergency coordination |
| PetSaathi operations owner | Booking, incident and service accountability |

PetSaathi should identify one authorised society contact and one backup contact.

5. Recommended partnership models

Model A — Referral partnership

The society permits PetSaathi promotion to residents.

Society shares service information

Residents book individually

PetSaathi handles payment and service

Society receives aggregate updates only

Best for

First pilot

Smaller societies

Low legal and financial complexity

Demand validation

Model B — Resident-benefit partnership

Residents receive a society-specific offer.

Examples:

₹99 first walk

Reduced package price

Priority support

Free first meet-and-greet

Same-sitter preference

PetSaathi still contracts and collects payment from individual customers.

Best for

Medium and premium societies

Repeat walking programmes

Simple commercial structure

Model C — Managed society pet-care desk

PetSaathi operates approved local service windows.

Example:

Morning walks: 6:30–9:00

Evening walks: 5:00–8:00

Dedicated local sitter roster

Daily operations owner

Society-specific support contact

Best for

High pet-parent density

Multiple daily walks

Strong society cooperation

Proven demand

Model D — Society-paid programme

The RWA, developer or property-management company pays a monthly amount for defined services.

Possible inclusions:

Resident helpline

Sitter-access management

Emergency coordination

Pet-parent onboarding sessions

Monthly service credits

Pet-care events

Aggregate safety reporting

Important

Define whether this is:

A sponsorship

A service retainer

A resident subsidy

A minimum booking commitment

A corporate amenity

Do not leave payment responsibility ambiguous.

6. Recommended Phase 7 partnership package

Resident services

Dog walking

Pet sitting

Controlled boarding referrals

Same-sitter repeat booking

Five/ten-walk packs

Festival and travel care

Trust features

Verified sitter profile

Service permissions

Society-access approval

Pet-care training badges

Photo/video updates

Automated Report Cards

Basic GPS walk proof

Emergency escalation

Society controls

Approved sitter registry

Entry and exit process

Service-hour configuration

Replacement approval process

Incident escalation contact

Society-specific booking code

Aggregate monthly dashboard

What the society should not access

The society should not automatically receive:

Resident phone numbers

Exact individual booking instructions

Pet medical information

Emergency family contacts

Payment details

Individual complaint content

Full GPS routes

Private photos or videos

The DPDP Act establishes a framework for lawful processing of digital personal data, and the final DPDP Rules were published in November 2025 with staged commencement provisions. PetSaathi should apply purpose limitation, appropriate access, resident notices and controlled retention to society-level data sharing.

The society dashboard should therefore be aggregated by default.

8. Step 1 — Select the right societies

Do not approach every residential property.

Recommended qualification criteria

### Table 2

| Criterion | Why it matters |
| --- | --- |
| 300+ occupied homes, or strong pet density | Sufficient demand potential |
| Active RWA/AOA or facility team | Clear decision-maker |
| Controlled security access | Partnership creates operational value |
| Existing pet-parent group | Faster activation |
| Local sitter supply | Service can actually be fulfilled |
| Nearby vet/emergency support | Better incident readiness |
| Suitable walking environment | Practical dog-walking operation |
| Committee willingness | Reduced implementation friction |

A smaller society with 30 active pet parents may be more valuable than a large society with low interest.

9. Step 2 — Society discovery survey

Before proposing a contract, collect:

Number of occupied homes

Estimated pet-owning households

Dogs/cats/other pets

Current walking arrangements

Common service times

Travel-care demand

Current sitter or domestic-help use

Access restrictions

Pet-related complaints

Resident language preferences

Interest in packages

Do not ask the committee to hand over a list of pet owners without an appropriate resident-facing notice or lawful basis.

Use an opt-in survey:

“I am interested in PetSaathi society services.”

11. Step 4 — Pilot agreement

The agreement should clearly state:

Society is not the pet-care provider.

PetSaathi controls sitter selection and service operations.

Residents remain responsible for accurate pet information.

The society does not guarantee sitter performance.

PetSaathi does not guarantee zero risk.

Individual payments remain between PetSaathi and customers unless otherwise agreed.

Emergency coordination is not veterinary diagnosis.

Also define:

Contract term

Termination rights

Branding approval

Promotional access

Dispute escalation

Liability allocation

Indemnity and insurance, after legal review

Applicable local and state requirements

Because society structures and by-laws vary by state and property type, the partnership template should receive local legal review before being used across India.

13. Step 6 — Local sitter network

Do not launch resident marketing before adequate sitter supply exists.

Recommended initial roster per society cluster

One sitter should not become the only person capable of serving the society.

Society sitter badge

Use:

Society access approved

This means:

Their identity is registered with security.

Required access steps are complete.

The approval is active.

Expiry or revocation is tracked.

It does not mean the society guarantees every action of the sitter.

The society should see aggregate information such as:

PetSaathi — Green Meadows

17. Commercial models

Resident-paid

Resident pays PetSaathi

PetSaathi pays sitter

Society may receive no fee or disclosed referral fee

Best for the first pilot.

Minimum monthly guarantee

Society guarantees minimum programme revenue

Residents receive subsidised pricing

Useful only after proven demand.

Monthly management retainer

The society pays for:

Local operations

Access registry

Support desk

Resident events

Aggregate reporting

Individual care services may still be resident-paid.

Revenue share

Use cautiously.

A revenue share can create:

Complex accounting

Higher resident prices

Disputes about refunds

Committee transparency concerns

Incentives to promote unsuitable services

Any fee or commission should be disclosed contractually and accounted for clearly.

Initial scope

One city

Two or three nearby societies

Dog walking as hero service

Pet sitting active

Twenty to thirty trained sitters across the cluster

Daily operational monitoring

45–60 days

The first two weeks should use a booking cap.

| Stage | Suggested cap |

| Weeks 3–4 | 15–25/day |

20. Major risks

One committee member is not full approval

Secure written, authorised approval according to the society’s governance process.

Committee changes

A new committee may review or cancel the partnership. Store agreement term, authorised signatories and termination process.

Access approval is mistaken for safety guarantee

Keep factual verification language and clear responsibility boundaries.

Resident data is overshared

Provide aggregate reports and role-restricted access.

Society exclusivity is accepted too early

Avoid long exclusive contracts before demand and operational quality are proven.

One sitter dominates the society

Maintain backup coverage and prevent single-sitter dependency.

Non-pet residents feel ignored

Provide cleanliness, common-area and complaint procedures without creating discriminatory restrictions against lawful pet ownership or community-animal care.

Society services are confused with community-dog management

Keep commercial pet services contractually separate from ABC/community-animal coordination.

21. What is wrong in many society launches

Mistake 1 — Signing partnerships without resident demand

Correct:

Survey → waitlist → pilot bookings → contract expansion

Mistake 2 — Treating the RWA as the customer and ignoring residents

The committee approves access, but residents decide whether the service is valuable.

Mistake 3 — Marketing before sitter supply

Do not create 100 resident leads with only one available walker.

Mistake 4 — No security-gate workflow

A booking is not operationally confirmed if the sitter cannot enter.

Mistake 5 — Sharing too much data with the society

Aggregate metrics should be the default.

Mistake 6 — No non-pet-resident communication

Explain:

Sitter access rules

Waste-cleanup expectations

Service hours

Complaint route

Emergency escalation

Mistake 7 — Promising society-wide emergency veterinary care

PetSaathi may coordinate listed contacts; it should not claim veterinary or ambulance capability it does not control.

22. Phase 7 definition of done

Phase 7 is ready for broader society expansion only when:

Partnership

Agreements are authorised and documented.

Commercial terms are clear.

Data sharing is limited.

Termination and dispute rules exist.

Operations

Society access procedures work.

Local sitter supply is sufficient.

Backup coverage exists.

Daily service windows are manageable.

Security staff understand the process.

Customer

Residents can register and book easily.

Pet Profiles and payments work.

Report Cards are delivered.

Same-sitter repeats work.

Support is responsive.

Safety

Society partnerships do not bypass PetSaathi risk controls.

Incidents remain structured and auditable.

Community-animal responsibilities are not misrepresented.

Sensitive data is role-restricted.

Emergency communication is tested.

Economics

Booking density improves.

Travel time falls.

Repeat demand is demonstrated.

Contribution margin is positive or moving toward it.

Support workload remains sustainable.

Final Phase 7 operating model

Select 2–3 suitable societies

↓

Validate resident demand

↓

Sign controlled pilot agreement

↓

Configure access and data rules

↓

Prepare local sitter and backup roster

↓

Launch trial bookings

↓

Convert successful trials into repeat plans

↓

Track density, quality and economics

↓

Renew successful societies

↓

Expand to nearby society clusters

Final operating principle

The society partnership is valuable only when it improves trust, local booking density and operational reliability. A signed agreement without active residents, nearby sitters and repeat services is not a moat.

Simple explanation for professor

“Phase 7 focuses on partnering with apartment societies, RWAs and gated communities.

Instead of accepting random bookings from different parts of a city, PetSaathi will build a local network inside selected societies. The society will help with resident communication and sitter access, while PetSaathi will remain responsible for booking, payment, sitter assignment, tracking, Report Cards and incidents.

This model can reduce travel time and customer-acquisition cost because many pet parents live in one place. It also makes daily walking plans and same-sitter bookings easier.

The process starts by selecting societies with enough pet-owner demand. PetSaathi will conduct a resident survey, meet the society committee, sign a pilot agreement, register approved sitters with security and launch limited trial bookings.

The society dashboard will show aggregate information such as completed services and on-time rates, but it will not show private resident medical, payment or emergency information.

We will first test the system in two or three nearby societies for 45 to 60 days. Success will be measured through paid residents, bookings per society, on-time services, Report Cards, repeat bookings, travel time, customer rating and positive unit economics.

The main objective is to create a trusted local pet-care network that can serve many residents reliably without expanding randomly across the whole city.”

PetSaathi Phase 7 — Legal Context, Duration and Society Targets 🏢🐾

Executive decision

Your central positioning is approved:

PetSaathi helps pet parents, non-pet residents, security teams and society management coexist safely through verified pet-care support, clear operating procedures and fast issue escalation.

Do not position PetSaathi as:

Controlling pets inside societies

Removing “problem dogs”

Making societies pet-free

Enforcing blanket pet restrictions

Replacing municipal community-animal responsibilities

Guaranteeing that a sitter or pet is completely risk-free

Phase 7 should run for 60 days in one city, but the proposed goal of 20–50 societies needs clarification:

20–50 societies can be the qualified outreach pipeline.

5–10 societies can be signed pilots.

3–5 societies should become fully operational during the first 60 days.

Trying to activate 20–50 societies simultaneously would likely reduce service quality, sitter density and operational control.

1. Verified legal position

Blanket pet bans

The AWBI’s 2015 guidelines state that RWAs and apartment-owner associations cannot introduce a ban on keeping pet dogs. The guidelines also reject restrictions based merely on dog size or a majority vote of residents.

BMC’s current published guidelines expressly repeat that RWAs and AOAs cannot impose bans on keeping pet dogs or cats, cannot treat barking alone as a sufficient ground for a ban, and cannot create such a ban merely through majority approval.

Important legal nuance

Do not market the rule as:

“Every RWA pet restriction anywhere in India is automatically illegal.”

A safer and more accurate statement is:

AWBI guidance states that blanket pet bans by RWAs and AOAs are not permissible. BMC expressly adopts that position in Mumbai. PetSaathi will review relevant state, municipal and society requirements before each city launch.

AWBI guidance is an important official animal-welfare position, but city-specific municipal requirements, state cooperative-housing laws, court orders, licences and valid conduct rules may still differ.

2. What societies may reasonably regulate

Pet ownership and responsible conduct must be treated separately.

A society should not prohibit residents from keeping pets, but reasonable operational arrangements may cover matters such as:

Common-area cleanliness

Waste collection

Service timings

Security entry

Leash and handling expectations

Alternate lift usage where available

Vaccination or municipal licence evidence where legally required

Response to a specific nuisance or safety event

BMC’s guidance states that pets cannot be excluded from lifts, although residents may be advised to use an accessible alternate lift. It also supports mutually acceptable walking timings rather than a complete park or walking ban.

For Mumbai specifically, BMC also requires dog licensing, advises timely vaccination and leashing in public areas, and places responsibility on owners or dog walkers to clean up pet waste. These exact requirements must not be assumed for every Indian city without local verification.

PetSaathi product implication

The society configuration should support:

Pet ownership permitted

+

Responsible conduct rules

+

Security-access procedure

+

Incident escalation

It should not support:

Pet ownership banned

Pet size banned

All pets banned from lifts

All pets banned from walking areas

Pet owner forced to surrender animal

3. Pet animals and community animals must remain separate

This distinction is critical.

Resident pets

These have an identified owner responsible for:

Accurate Pet Profile information

Health and vaccination information

Safe equipment

Waste cleanup

Handling instructions

Service booking and payment

Community animals

These are animals for which no individual ownership has been claimed. The Animal Birth Control Rules, 2023 and later AWBI guidance address community-animal feeding, sterilisation, vaccination and conflict-resolution arrangements.

AWBI’s July 2025 guidance states that RWA, AOA or local-body representatives have responsibilities around structured feeding provisions for community animals. It also directs maintenance of information such as estimated population, sterilisation status, anti-rabies vaccination records and identified feeders or caregivers.

Product boundary

PetSaathi’s commercial society partnership should cover:

Resident pet services

Sitter access

Bookings

Payments

Dog walking

Pet sitting

Report Cards

Service incidents

A separate optional module may later cover:

Community-animal register

Feeding-point coordination

Vaccination-drive coordination

Municipal or NGO referrals

PetSaathi must not imply that a paid dog-walking partnership replaces the society’s responsibilities relating to community animals.

4. Approved society messaging

Recommended primary message

PetSaathi helps societies create a safer and more organised pet-care environment through verified caregivers, controlled entry, service updates, Report Cards and responsive support.

Resident-facing version

Book trusted local pet-care support within your society, with verified caregiver profiles, photo updates, walk proof, Report Cards and emergency escalation.

Committee-facing version

PetSaathi provides a structured resident pet-care programme with approved-access caregivers, booking records, service monitoring, incident handling and aggregate society reporting.

Non-pet-resident version

The programme supports responsible service timings, controlled sitter entry, waste-cleanup expectations and a clear complaint process while respecting lawful pet ownership.

Avoid these phrases

“Pet control programme”

“Pet menace solution”

“Dog-free society”

“Guaranteed safe pets”

“Zero-risk walkers”

“RWA-certified safe sitter”

“Removal of community dogs”

“Ban difficult breeds”

Use factual badges instead:

Identity verified

Training completed

Society access registered

Large-dog handling approved

Police-verification document reviewed

5. Resident-data protection

A society should not casually send PetSaathi a spreadsheet containing resident names, telephone numbers, flat numbers and pet information.

The safer acquisition process is:

Society shares QR code or announcement

↓

Interested resident opens PetSaathi form

↓

Resident receives privacy notice

↓

Resident voluntarily registers

↓

PetSaathi records consent and purpose

The Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 establishes a framework for lawful processing of digital personal data. The final DPDP Rules, 2025 were published with staged commencement dates, so PetSaathi should map society onboarding, notices, security, grievance handling and retention against the provisions applicable at launch.

Society dashboard rule

Show aggregate information by default:

Registered residents: 28

Completed services: 73

On-time rate: 96%

Average rating: 4.7

Open access issues: 1

Do not expose:

Individual pet medical information

Full customer phone numbers

Emergency contacts

Exact booking instructions

Full GPS routes

Payment data

Private photos or videos

Complaint narratives

6. Phase 7 duration decision

### Table 6

| Version | Assessment |
| --- | --- |
| 15–20 days | Suitable for discovery, meetings and a small proof of concept |
| 45–60 days | Best for signing, activating and evaluating society pilots |
| 90 days | Better for renewals, repeat-plan maturity and deeper city clustering |

Approved duration

Run a 60-day society partnership phase in one city.

Twenty days is usually insufficient to measure:

Repeat-plan conversion

Package-credit usage

Sitter reliability

Committee satisfaction

Resident referrals

Partnership renewal interest

Ninety days may later be used to deepen a successful cluster, but it should not delay the first controlled pilot.

7. Correction to the “20–50 societies” target

Wrong interpretation

Launch operational services in 20–50 societies within 60 days

This would require:

Large sitter supply

Many security onboarding processes

Multiple committee negotiations

Distributed support coverage

Society-specific operating procedures

More replacement capacity

It would recreate the same low-density problem that Phase 7 is meant to solve.

Correct interpretation

Build a list of 50 qualified societies

↓

Contact 40–50

↓

Complete 15–20 meetings

↓

Identify 8–12 interested societies

↓

Sign 5–10 pilot agreements

↓

Fully activate 3–5 clustered societies

The next group should be activated only after the first cluster demonstrates stable operations.

9. Success-target analysis

Societies contacted — 50+

Approve.

This should mean qualified outreach, not mass unsolicited messaging.

Society meetings — 15–20

Approve.

This represents a 30–40% meeting rate from 50 qualified targets, which is ambitious but possible with warm introductions, local networks and targeted outreach.

Interested societies — 8–12

Approve with definition.

“Interested” should require:

Identified decision-maker

Confirmed resident demand

Operational feasibility

Willingness to review pilot terms

Suitable service cluster

A committee member saying “send details” is not a qualified opportunity.

Signed or pilot societies — 5–10

Approve as an ambitious range.

Use:

Minimum success: 5

Strong result: 8

Stretch target: 10

Only 3–5 need to be fully activated during the initial 60 days.

Society pet-parent leads — 200–500

Approve only for opt-in leads.

Define a valid lead as a resident who has:

Submitted the society registration form

Provided contact information voluntarily

Identified a pet or service need

Accepted the relevant privacy notice

Do not count poster views or WhatsApp-group members as leads.

Paid bookings — 100–300

Approve, but define whether this means total booking transactions or unique customers.

Across five pilot societies:

100 bookings = 20 bookings per society over 60 days

300 bookings = 60 bookings per society over 60 days

Across ten pilot societies:

100 bookings = 10 bookings per society

300 bookings = 30 bookings per society

A society with three one-time bookings is not yet a strong partnership.

Add the density metric:

Completed bookings per active society per week

Monthly repeat plans — 20–50

Approve as the strongest commercial validation target.

Track:

Plans purchased

Credits issued

Credits used

Plan completion

Same-sitter fulfilment

Cancellation

Expired credits

The real success metric is not only plan sales:

Plan credits successfully consumed

Society revenue — ₹50,000 to ₹2 lakh per month

Modify the definition.

“Society revenue” is ambiguous. Track four separate values:

Society-sourced GMV

PetSaathi platform revenue

Gross contribution

Net operating contribution

For example:

50 plans × ₹1,499

= ₹74,950 monthly GMV

At a 30% platform gross margin:

PetSaathi gross margin

= approximately ₹22,485

before support and operating costs

Therefore:

₹50,000–₹2 lakh monthly GMV is achievable with moderate booking density.

₹50,000–₹2 lakh monthly platform revenue requires substantially more bookings, higher-value sitting/boarding services or society retainers.

The report must not call GMV “revenue.”

Recommended target

End-of-phase monthly society-sourced GMV:

₹50,000–₹2 lakh

Positive contribution margin:

Required for strongest societies

Platform net revenue:

Reported separately

Customer rating — 4.6+

Approve with sample-size protection.

Report:

Average rating

Number of reviews

Review response rate

Rating distribution

Service type

Society

A 4.8 rating from five customers is not equivalent to 4.8 from one hundred customers.

Society complaints — low/manageable

Reject the vague wording and replace it with measurable rules.

Recommended metrics:

### Table 7

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Critical unresolved society complaints | 0 |
| Security-entry failures | Below 3% of bookings |
| Society complaints | Below 5 per 100 bookings |
| Complaint acknowledgement | Same day |
| Active-service complaint response | Under 10 minutes |
| Repeated unresolved complaint pattern | 0 |
| Complaints with owner and status | 100% |

Classify complaints as:

Access

Cleanliness

Noise

Sitter conduct

Common-area use

Community-animal conflict

Privacy

Safety

Billing

10. Revised Phase 7 success scorecard

### Table 8

| Metric | Minimum | Strong | Stretch |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Qualified societies contacted | 40 | 50 | 75 |
| Meetings completed | 12 | 15–20 | 25 |
| Qualified opportunities | 6 | 8–12 | 15 |
| Signed pilots | 5 | 8 | 10 |

| Critical unresolved complaints | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| Society-sourced monthly GMV | ₹50k | ₹1L | ₹2L |
| Positive contribution societies | 1 | 3 | 5 |

11. Additional success metrics

Add these to prevent vanity reporting.

Activation rate

Societies producing at least five paid bookings

÷ signed pilot societies

Resident conversion

Residents completing first paid booking

÷ opted-in resident leads

Density

Completed bookings

÷ active society

÷ week

Repeat completion

Customers completing a second paid service

÷ customers whose repeat window has matured

Sitter travel improvement

Phase 5 median travel time

versus

Phase 7 society-cluster travel time

Society health

Renewal interest

Committee satisfaction

Security-entry success

Resident complaint rate

Non-pet-resident complaint rate

12. Phase 8 go/no-go recommendation

Move to Phase 8 only when:

### Table 9

| Requirement | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Fully active pilot societies | 3–5+ |
| Society bookings | 100–300 |
| On-time rate | 95%+ |
| Report Cards delivered | 98%+ |
| Repeat-plan customers | 20–50 |
| Critical unresolved society complaints | 0 |
| Security-entry success | 97%+ |
| Customer rating | 4.6+ with meaningful sample |
| Positive contribution | Demonstrated in strongest cluster |
| Society renewal interest | Majority of active pilots |
| Sitter backup coverage | Stable |
| Privacy and access SOP | Operational |

Decision options

MOVE_TO_PHASE_8

DEEPEN_CURRENT_SOCIETY_CLUSTER

FIX_PARTNERSHIP_OPERATIONS

CHANGE_SOCIETY_SEGMENT

CHANGE_SERVICE_MIX

REPEAT_PHASE_7

Final approved recommendation

Run:

One city, 50 qualified society targets, 15–20 meetings, 5–10 signed pilots and 3–5 fully activated society partnerships over 60 days.

The operating position should be:

PetSaathi supports lawful pet ownership, responsible pet-care practices, controlled sitter access, transparent service proof and constructive coexistence between pet parents, other residents and society management.

The Phase 7 win condition is not:

50 society logos on the website

It is:

Several nearby societies

+

Active resident customers

+

Reliable local sitters

+

Repeat service plans

+

Fewer travel and access failures

+

Positive contribution

+

Low, well-managed complaints

Legal review remains necessary before using a common agreement or policy across different states and municipal jurisdictions.

A monthly AWBI, municipal and RWA-policy change watch would be useful before expanding this programme beyond the first city.

PetSaathi Phase 7 — Positioning and Society Service Model 🏢🐾

Executive decision

Your positioning is strong, but the official offer should distinguish pet-care services, veterinary coordination, partner-led events, and optional community features.

Recommended official positioning

Verified pet-care support for apartment communities—dog walking, in-home pet sitting, screened boarding, service updates, emergency veterinary coordination and pet-parent community support.

Committee-facing version

PetSaathi helps residents access safe, verified and reliable pet-care services while supporting organised sitter access, transparent service proof, responsive issue handling and smoother coexistence within the community.

Avoid

“We manage all pets in your society.”

That wording suggests that PetSaathi or the RWA controls residents’ animals. AWBI guidance states that RWAs and apartment-owner associations should not impose blanket bans on residents keeping pet dogs, while the Animal Birth Control Rules separately address the management of community animals.

1. Revised Phase 7 service model

### Table 10

| Service | Final status | Important condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Daily dog walking | Active—core | Approved-access sitter, Pet Profile and Report Card |
| Pet sitting at home | Active—core | Strong address, key and privacy controls |
| Controlled pet boarding | Restricted beta | Screened hosts, separate compatibility review |
| Emergency veterinary coordination | Active—coordination only | No diagnosis or treatment by PetSaathi |
| Grooming camp | Partner-led pilot | Qualified partner and premises approval |
| Vaccination camp | Registered-veterinarian partner only | Medical consent and veterinary records |
| Pet training workshop | Partner-led pilot | Humane methods and screened trainer |
| Lost-pet alert | Basic controlled feature | Time-limited, privacy-safe alert |
| Pet-parent community | Active—moderated | Not an emergency or medical channel |
| Society pet directory | Optional opt-in | Private, minimal and purpose-limited |

2. Daily dog walking

Decision: Active and primary society service

Dog walking should be the hero offering because it creates:

Repeat demand

Predictable service windows

Same-sitter continuity

High society-level booking density

Easier package conversion

Required operating flow

Resident creates Pet Profile

↓

Dog-walking risk assessment completed

↓

Society-approved sitter assigned

↓

Payment verified

↓

Security entry prepared

↓

Sitter arrives and starts walk

↓

Basic GPS proof + media

↓

Pet returned safely

↓

Report Card delivered

Society-specific controls

Record:

Approved gate

Entry procedure

Visitor pass requirement

Permitted service timings

Lift/common-area instructions

Pet handover point

Waste-cleanup expectations

Replacement-sitter procedure

A society badge should state “Society access registered”, not “Society guarantees this sitter.”

3. Pet sitting at home

Decision: Active with stronger privacy controls

Pet sitting involves access to the resident’s home and therefore requires more protection than dog walking.

Required controls

Exact address visible only after final assignment

Access instructions released only when operationally necessary

Key or smart-lock handover logged

Entry and exit confirmation

Private media storage

Relevant emergency contacts

Customer-defined home restrictions

Medication tasks limited to approved instructions and sitter capability

Access revoked immediately after completion or sitter removal

The society should not see:

Apartment access codes

Interior photographs

Pet medication details

Customer emergency contacts

Private sitter notes

Full service history

4. Controlled pet boarding

Decision: Restricted beta

Use the term:

Screened boarding beta

rather than implying that every society or sitter can provide boarding.

Boarding requirements

Pet Profile complete

↓

Current health declaration

↓

Vaccination records reviewed according to policy

↓

Other-animal compatibility reviewed

↓

Host and property approved

↓

Feeding, sleeping and medication instructions

↓

Emergency contact and veterinary plan

↓

Meet-and-greet where required

↓

Payment/deposit

Boarding host controls

Maximum pet capacity

Resident-animal information

Safe separation area

Escape prevention

Feeding separation

Cleaning process

Emergency transport plan

Local veterinarian

Daily Report Cards

Backup host or alternative-care plan

Property-owner/society permission where applicable

Do not operate boarding from society common areas.

Premises, trade, animal-welfare and local municipal requirements can differ by city and state, so every boarding location needs local legal and operational review before activation.

5. Emergency veterinary coordination

Decision: Active, but rename carefully

Use:

Emergency veterinary coordination

Avoid:

“Emergency veterinary treatment by PetSaathi.”

PetSaathi may:

Display verified veterinary contacts

Contact the owner

Contact an emergency clinic

Arrange transport through an approved provider

Share authorised pet information

Record the emergency timeline

Assist with owner spending authorisation

Coordinate sitter handover

PetSaathi should not:

Diagnose a condition

Prescribe medication

Change medication doses

Promise ambulance availability without a contracted provider

Guarantee veterinary response time

Represent support staff as veterinary professionals

The Indian Veterinary Council Act regulates veterinary practice, and the right to practise veterinary medicine is tied to registered veterinary practitioners, subject to limited statutory exceptions.

Emergency workflow

Concern detected

↓

Incident ticket created

↓

Owner contacted

↓

Registered veterinary partner contacted

↓

Transport or clinic instructions confirmed

↓

Authorisation recorded

↓

Outcome and records linked to booking

6. Grooming camps

Decision: Partner-led pilot

PetSaathi should organise and coordinate the event, while a screened grooming provider performs the service.

Required controls

Written society permission

Groomer identity and business verification

Service menu and pricing

Resident consent

Pet health and behaviour declaration

Hygiene and equipment plan

Water, electricity and waste-disposal plan

Appointment capacity

Incident procedure

Cancellation/refund rules

No sedation by a groomer

Veterinary escalation for health concerns

Recommended Phase 7 scope

Start with:

Bathing

Brushing

Basic coat care

Nail trimming where appropriate

Ear-cleaning only within partner competence

Avoid complex or medically sensitive procedures during the initial pilot.

7. Vaccination camps

Decision: Registered-veterinarian partner only

PetSaathi should act as:

Event coordinator

+

Booking and communication platform

+

Record-delivery facilitator

The veterinary partner remains responsible for clinical decisions and vaccine administration.

Required controls

Registered veterinary practitioner

Veterinary registration details checked

Owner consent

Pet identity confirmation

Current medical-history questions

Vaccine name

Batch and expiry information

Administration date

Next-due date

Veterinary certificate

Storage and handling under veterinary-partner procedures

Adverse-event escalation

Emergency equipment and contact plan

Waste and sharps disposal by the appropriate partner

The Veterinary Council of India maintains the governing professional framework and code of conduct for registered veterinary practitioners.

Do not advertise:

“All pets must be vaccinated at the PetSaathi camp.”

Participation should be voluntary unless a separate lawful service requirement applies.

8. Pet training workshops

Decision: Partner-led educational programme

Initial workshops may cover:

Basic leash handling

Recall foundations

Puppy socialisation

Separation preparation

Enrichment

Safe introductions

Dog-body-language education

Responsible common-area behaviour

Partner screening

Record:

Trainer identity

Training experience

Methods used

Species and behaviour scope

References

Incident history

Insurance where available

Prohibited methods

Escalation to veterinarian or qualified behaviour professional

Safety rules

Do not permit:

Physical cruelty

Fear-based public demonstrations

Unsupervised dog-to-dog mixing

Guaranteed behaviour “cures”

Medical diagnosis

Automatic breed-based judgement

Group sessions without compatibility controls

9. Lost-pet alert

Decision: Basic controlled feature

This can provide strong society-level value, but it creates privacy and misuse risks.

Alert fields

Alert ID

Pet name

Current photograph

Species and description

Last-seen society area

Last-seen time

Safe approach instructions

Owner-approved contact method

Alert status

Expiry time

Privacy protections

Do not publish:

Full apartment number

Owner’s private phone number by default

Detailed home-access information

Pet medical records

Permanent public location history

Use:

Masked contact

In-app response

Society security contact

Time-limited alert link

Status flow

DRAFT

ACTIVE

SIGHTING_REPORTED

OWNER_CONTACTED

FOUND

EXPIRED

CANCELLED

FALSE_REPORT_REVIEW

Critical boundary

The lost-pet feature must not be used to identify and relocate community animals. Community-animal management is governed separately under the Animal Birth Control Rules and AWBI implementation guidance.

10. Pet-parent community

Decision: Active with moderation

Recommended functions:

Society announcements

Pet-care events

Sitter introductions

Veterinary-camp announcements

Walking-group coordination

Responsible pet-care education

Lost-pet alerts

Resident referrals

Do not use it as

An emergency-support channel

A veterinary diagnosis group

A place for publishing resident complaints or allegations

A public pet-risk list

A replacement for structured incident reporting

A channel for sharing private resident data

Moderation categories

Harassment

Personal-data disclosure

Dangerous medical advice

Animal cruelty

False lost-pet reports

Commercial spam

Resident disputes

Discriminatory pet/breed targeting

Every emergency message should redirect to:

Call PetSaathi support

or

Open an incident ticket

11. Society pet directory

Decision: Optional and opt-in only

A pet directory should never be created automatically from resident booking data.

The DPDP Act and the final DPDP Rules establish requirements around digital personal-data processing, including clear notices, security and staged statutory obligations. PetSaathi should therefore collect directory participation separately from service registration.

Safe directory entry

A resident may choose to display:

Pet name

Pet photograph

Species

General breed description

Pet-parent display name

Society block, optionally

Friendly-with-dogs status as owner-reported

Participation in pet events

Never display publicly

Exact apartment number

Personal phone number

Medical conditions

Bite history

Risk classification

Veterinary records

Vaccination documents

Emergency contacts

Booking history

Home-access information

Access levels

PRIVATE

VERIFIED_SOCIETY_RESIDENTS

PET_PARENT_GROUP_ONLY

HIDDEN

Required customer controls

Explicit opt-in

Preview before publication

Edit

Hide temporarily

Delete

Report misuse

Separate marketing consent

12. Partner-service architecture

Create a partner registry:

society_service_partners

- id

- partner_type

- business_name

- city

- verification_status

- professional_registration

- insurance_reference

- service_scope

- agreement_start

- agreement_end

- incident_contact

- status

Partner types:

VETERINARY_CLINIC

VETERINARY_PRACTITIONER

GROOMER

TRAINER

BOARDING_HOST

PET_TRANSPORT

EMERGENCY_PROVIDER

Partner statuses

APPLIED

UNDER_REVIEW

APPROVED

PROBATION

ACTIVE

SUSPENDED

EXPIRED

REJECTED

PetSaathi should clearly disclose when a service is supplied by a third-party partner. Pricing, refund, grievance and service information must be displayed clearly; India’s e-commerce rules require relevant consumer information and handling of accepted refunds.

13. Final service priority

P0 — Launch with every active society

Daily dog walking

In-home pet sitting

Emergency veterinary coordination

Pet-parent community

Lost-pet alert

Society sitter-access system

Report Cards and incident support

P1 — Add after stable initial operations

Controlled boarding beta

Same-sitter walking plans

Grooming camp

Training workshop

P2 — Add with professional partners and stronger controls

Vaccination camp

Society pet directory

Advanced community events

Pet transport partnerships

Vaccination can occur earlier when a fully verified veterinary partner and operational plan already exist, but PetSaathi should not rush medical events merely for society promotion.

14. What would be wrong in this model

Mistake 1 — Calling coordination “veterinary support”

Problem: Customers may believe PetSaathi provides medical advice.

Correction: Use “emergency veterinary coordination through registered partners.”

Mistake 2 — Automatically creating a pet directory

Problem: Residents may not want pet identity and household information shared.

Correction: Separate, explicit opt-in with minimal data.

Mistake 3 — Treating boarding like ordinary sitting

Problem: Boarding creates compatibility, property, health and capacity risks.

Correction: Restricted beta with separate approval.

Mistake 4 — Letting camps operate without a verified partner

Problem: PetSaathi may unintentionally become responsible for activities outside its competence.

Correction: Partner-led service with written role allocation.

Mistake 5 — Sharing all service data with the society

Problem: Society approval does not justify unrestricted access to resident data.

Correction: Aggregate society reporting by default.

Mistake 6 — Using the community group for incident handling

Problem: Evidence becomes fragmented and allegations become public.

Correction: Community message → structured complaint or incident ticket.

Final approved positioning

PetSaathi provides verified pet-care support for apartment communities, including dog walking, in-home pet sitting, screened boarding, service updates, emergency veterinary coordination and pet-parent community support. We help residents access reliable care while supporting organised sitter entry, clear service records, responsive issue handling and responsible coexistence within the society.

Final operating principle

PetSaathi owns the care-service workflow; qualified partners own specialist services; residents control their personal data; and the society receives only the access and aggregate information necessary to operate the partnership.

Simple explanation for professor

“Phase 7 will provide pet-care services inside selected apartment societies. The main active services will be dog walking and home pet sitting. Boarding will remain a controlled beta because it needs more health, compatibility and property checks.

PetSaathi will provide emergency veterinary coordination, but it will not diagnose or treat animals. Medical services and vaccination camps will be conducted only through registered veterinary partners.

Grooming camps and training workshops will also be organised through verified partners. The lost-pet alert will help residents and security find an owner’s missing pet, but it will protect the owner’s private contact and apartment details.

The pet-parent community will support events, updates and referrals, but emergencies and serious complaints will always be recorded through the formal incident system.

A society pet directory will remain optional. Residents must separately choose whether their pet appears in it, and sensitive information such as medical records, bite history and exact apartment details will never be publicly displayed.

This approach gives residents convenient pet-care support without allowing the society or PetSaathi to control lawful pet ownership or expose private information.”

PetSaathi Phase 7 — Package 1: No-Fee Society Pilot 🏢🐾

Executive decision

The package is a strong entry offer for the first five societies, but the public name should be modified.

Recommended name

PetSaathi No-Fee Society Pilot

or:

Free Society Setup Pilot

Avoid advertising it simply as a “Free Society Pilot”, because the society pays no partnership fee but residents still pay for dog walking, pet sitting and other booked services. ASCI’s advertising code requires claims to be truthful and says something should not be described as “free” when the consumer must bear a direct cost other than limited delivery-type charges. The offer must therefore explain clearly what is free and what remains paid.

Correct headline

No setup or partnership fee for the society during the 15–30 day pilot. Residents pay only when they book an individual PetSaathi service.

1. Purpose of this package

The No-Fee Society Pilot is not intended to generate partnership revenue immediately.

Its purpose is to validate:

Resident demand

Society access procedures

Local sitter availability

Booking density

Customer trust

Repeat walking demand

Operational complaints

Society willingness to continue

The revenue model during the pilot is:

Society partnership fee: ₹0

Resident registration fee: ₹0

Pet Profile creation: ₹0

Individual care booking: Paid

Sitter payout: Per completed service

PetSaathi income: Margin from resident bookings

The package should be treated as a customer-acquisition and operating-validation programme, not unlimited free support.

2. Recommended package structure

### Table 11

| Feature | Included | Important boundary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Society onboarding | Yes | One standard setup |
| Resident trial offer | Yes | One eligible offer per household |
| Society-approved sitter roster | Yes | Only active, relevant sitters |
| WhatsApp booking assistance | Yes | Resident-initiated or opt-in |
| Pet-care awareness poster | Yes | Standard co-branded asset |
| Pet-parent group introduction | Yes | Moderated, optional group |
| Society landing page/QR | Yes | Recommended addition |
| Basic monthly pilot summary | Yes | Aggregated data only |
| Society branding | Minimal | Logo/name only with permission |
| Dedicated on-site desk | No | Paid package later |
| Guaranteed dedicated sitter | No | Depends on availability |
| Unlimited emergency service | No | Coordination only |
| Free resident pet-care services | No | Bookings remain paid |
| Custom software development | No | Standard pilot configuration |
| Exclusivity | No | Not during first pilot |

3. What “free” includes

The society receives the following without a partnership or setup fee:

Society setup

PetSaathi will:

Create the society account

Record authorised committee and facility contacts

Configure approved service hours

Document gate-entry procedures

Record replacement-sitter rules

Set a pilot booking cap

Create a society-specific booking link or QR code

Society sitter roster

PetSaathi will provide a limited list of sitters who are:

Active on the platform

Approved for relevant services

Available in the society’s area

Registered for society access where required

Trained for their assigned service categories

Use the term:

Society access registered

Do not use:

Society guarantees this sitter

The society should receive only the information needed for gate and access administration—not full sitter identity documents, private records or unrelated platform information.

Resident awareness materials

The pilot may include:

One digital poster

One printable lobby poster

One WhatsApp announcement

One FAQ

One safety/process overview

One resident booking QR code

Basic pilot reporting

The committee may receive aggregated figures such as:

Residents registered

Booking requests

Completed services

On-time service rate

Average resident rating

Open society-access issues

Resolved society-level complaints

Individual Pet Profiles, medical details, private media, full GPS routes, emergency contacts and payment information should not be exposed through the society report. The DPDP Rules, 2025 were officially published with an enforcement timeline, so PetSaathi should build resident notice, purpose limitation, controlled access and retention into society onboarding.

4. Resident trial offer

Recommended offer

For dog walking:

First 30-minute dog walk at ₹99–₹149, including sitter verification details, service updates and a Pet Report Card.

Alternative pet-sitting offer:

First one-hour home pet-sitting visit at ₹199–₹299.

Do not provide every service free. A fully free care service can:

Attract low-intent bookings

Increase no-shows

Reduce perceived trust

Create sitter-compensation problems

Make paid conversion harder to measure

Trial eligibility

Recommended conditions:

Verified society resident

One trial per household

One eligible pet

Complete Pet Profile

Service area active

Eligible sitter available

Advance payment completed

Not transferable

Subject to pilot booking cap

After-trial conversion

After a successful trial:

Report Card delivered

↓

Customer review requested

↓

Same-sitter option shown

↓

5-walk starter plan offered

↓

10-walk or monthly plan offered later

The trial should validate repeat demand, not remain an isolated discounted booking.

5. WhatsApp booking support

Approved use

WhatsApp may support:

Resident registration link

Booking assistance

Payment reminders

Sitter assignment notifications

Service-start updates

Report Card link

Replacement updates

Refund-status updates

Important controls

The society should not automatically add every resident to a PetSaathi WhatsApp group or share resident contact lists.

Use:

Society announcement

↓

Resident opens QR/link

↓

Resident receives privacy notice

↓

Resident opts in

↓

WhatsApp communication begins

The committee should not upload a resident database unless the necessary notice, authority and purpose have been properly established.

WhatsApp group boundary

The optional pet-parent group may be used for:

Service announcements

Pet-care events

Sitter introductions

Awareness content

Lost-pet alerts

General community coordination

It should not be used for:

Medical diagnosis

Public complaints against sitters

Incident investigation

Publishing risk classifications

Sharing apartment access details

Emergency response

Emergencies should move immediately into the formal support and incident workflow.

6. Pet-care awareness poster

Recommended content

The poster should explain:

Available services

Dog walking

In-home pet sitting

Screened boarding beta, where available

Emergency veterinary coordination

Service updates and Report Cards

Trust controls

Verified caregiver profiles

Controlled society entry

Pet Profile and care instructions

Service evidence

Support and incident escalation

Resident responsibilities

Provide accurate behaviour and medical information

Keep safe leash/harness equipment ready

Inform PetSaathi of health changes

Follow society cleanliness requirements

Keep emergency contacts updated

Call to action

Scan to register your pet and book the society trial.

Messaging boundary

The poster must not suggest that PetSaathi:

Controls all animals in the society

Guarantees zero risk

Replaces a veterinarian

Can remove community animals

Allows the society to prohibit lawful pet ownership

AWBI continues to publish official guidance for pet owners, caregivers, RWAs and apartment-owner associations. Society communication should support responsible coexistence rather than blanket pet-control language.

7. Pet-parent group introduction

Recommended introduction format

PetSaathi may conduct:

A 30–45 minute online orientation

A small society lobby session

A sitter-introduction meeting

A resident Q&A

A dog-walking demonstration without unsafe group mixing

Suggested agenda

1. What PetSaathi provides

2. How sitters are reviewed

3. How residents create Pet Profiles

4. How booking and payment work

5. How service tracking works

6. What the Report Card contains

7. What happens if a sitter cancels

8. How incidents and emergencies are handled

9. Trial offer and repeat plans

10. Questions from residents

Important inclusion

The meeting should include non-pet-resident concerns where relevant:

Entry control

Common-area behaviour

Waste cleanup

Walking timings

Complaint process

Privacy

This helps position the pilot as a community convenience and safety programme rather than an exclusive pet-owner promotion.

8. Minimal society branding

Included

PetSaathi may provide:

PetSaathi × Green Meadows

Society Pilot

on:

Resident poster

Landing page

WhatsApp announcement

Pilot summary

Society-specific QR page

Permission requirement

PetSaathi should obtain written approval before using:

Society logo

Society name in public advertising

Committee-member testimonial

Photographs of society premises

Resident testimonials

Claims that the society endorses or recommends PetSaathi

ASCI requires advertising claims and institutional references to be truthful and not misleading; references that create an unjustified advantage should have permission.

Not included

Full custom visual identity

Dedicated society app

White-label platform

Large physical banners

Permanent on-site kiosk

Custom campaign production

Exclusive co-branding

These can belong to a future paid package.

9. Pilot duration

Recommended default

21 days

Allow a range of:

Minimum: 15 days

Default: 21 days

Maximum: 30 days

Why 21 days works

It provides enough time for:

Resident announcement

First bookings

Report Card delivery

Initial reviews

Same-sitter repeat requests

Five-walk-plan interest

Committee feedback

A 15-day pilot may work for a high-intent society. A 30-day pilot is better where resident onboarding is slower.

Pilot phases

Days 1–3: Setup

Society configuration

Security briefing

Sitter access registration

Resident communication

Landing page and QR launch

Days 4–14: Trial services

First paid bookings

Daily monitoring

Complaint handling

Resident feedback

Sitter-performance tracking

Days 15–21: Conversion and review

Repeat-plan offers

Committee review

Pilot metrics

Continuation proposal

10. Society eligibility

The free pilot should not be offered to every society.

Recommended qualification requirements

A society should have:

A recognised committee or authorised facility manager

One operational contact

One security contact

A viable number of interested pet parents

Local sitter supply

Clear entry rules

Willingness to distribute the resident opt-in link

Agreement to the basic incident and access process

Suggested demand threshold

Before activation, aim for either:

10+ interested pet-parent households

or:

5+ likely paid trial bookings

A society with no resident interest should remain in the lead pipeline rather than consuming setup resources.

11. Recommended first-five rollout

Do not activate all five societies on the same day.

Safer sequence

Society 1

↓

First 5–10 services reviewed

↓

Society 2

↓

Operational corrections

↓

Societies 3–5 launched as a cluster

Select societies that are geographically close enough to share sitter capacity and backup coverage.

First-five selection mix

A useful pilot group could include:

Two large premium societies

Two medium family-oriented societies

One smaller high-pet-density society

The purpose is to learn which society profile produces the best conversion and operational density.

12. Pilot economics

Example: one society

Assume:

15 paid trial dog walks

Average customer price: ₹129

Total GMV: ₹1,935

Average sitter payout: ₹90

Total sitter payouts: ₹1,350

Payment/support cost: ₹180

Pre-acquisition contribution: ₹405

Add pilot acquisition costs:

Design and poster allocation

Operations setup

Security training

Resident orientation

Discount subsidy

Support time

The first pilot may be unprofitable. That is acceptable when it generates evidence and repeat plans.

Stronger economic outcome

Suppose five residents purchase ten-walk plans at ₹1,499:

Plan GMV: ₹7,495

If the gross platform margin is approximately 30%:

Gross platform margin: roughly ₹2,249

before support and acquisition costs

The key commercial metric is:

Contribution after resident conversion and actual service delivery—not the number of people joining the WhatsApp group.

15. What is wrong in the original package

Problem 1 — “Free” is ambiguous

Correction

Say:

No society setup or partnership fee. Resident bookings are paid.

Problem 2 — “Verified sitter list” may expose too much data

Correction

Provide an active society-access roster with minimum necessary fields.

Problem 3 — WhatsApp support could become unlimited manual booking work

Correction

Define support hours, supported requests and escalation channels.

Problem 4 — No demand threshold

Correction

Require minimum resident interest before full setup.

Problem 5 — No booking cap

Correction

Set a pilot cap based on local sitter capacity.

Problem 6 — No conversion plan

Correction

Use trial → repeat booking → five-walk plan → monthly programme.

Problem 7 — Free pilot may continue indefinitely

Correction

Use a fixed end date, one review and an explicit continuation decision.

Final approved package

PetSaathi No-Fee Society Pilot

Best for

The first five qualified societies in one city cluster.

Duration

15–30 days, with 21 days recommended.

Society price

Setup fee: ₹0

Pilot partnership fee: ₹0

Automatic renewal: No

Resident price

Residents pay only for the individual services they book.

Included

Society onboarding

Society access configuration

Resident trial offer

Society-access sitter roster

WhatsApp booking assistance

Society landing page and QR

Basic awareness materials

Pet-parent introduction

Minimal approved co-branding

Aggregated pilot report

Revenue

PetSaathi earns from completed resident bookings and subsequent walking or sitting plans.

Main success condition

The pilot should create paid services, high resident satisfaction and repeat demand—not merely registrations or WhatsApp-group members.

Simple explanation for professor

“The first society package will be a no-fee pilot for the first five qualified apartment societies.

The society will not pay any setup or partnership fee for approximately 15 to 30 days. However, resident pet-care services will not be completely free. Residents will pay when they book dog walking or pet sitting, usually with a discounted first-service offer.

PetSaathi will configure the society, document security-entry rules, provide an approved sitter roster, create a QR booking page, share awareness material and introduce the service to pet parents.

Residents will register voluntarily rather than the society sharing their private contact list. The society will receive only aggregate results such as the number of completed services and the on-time rate. It will not receive individual medical, payment or GPS information.

The pilot will have a fixed booking cap and end date. After the pilot, PetSaathi will review paid bookings, customer ratings, repeat demand, entry issues and complaints. Successful societies can then move to a paid or long-term partnership package.”

etSaathi Phase 7 — Package 2: Basic Society Partner 🏢🐾

Executive decision

The Basic Society Partner package is commercially sensible after a successful pilot, but two parts need correction:

The ₹5,000–₹15,000 monthly range should be tied to society size, active pet-parent demand, support intensity and reporting needs.

Replace the vague phrase “plus commission on bookings” with a clearly defined commercial model.

Recommended public positioning

A managed pet-care partnership for apartment communities, including a society-specific resident portal, local sitter access, priority matching, resident benefits, monthly operational reporting and dedicated support.

Recommended commercial wording

Society partnership fee: ₹5,000–₹15,000 per month, plus applicable taxes. Residents pay separately for the pet-care services they book. PetSaathi earns its standard platform margin from completed bookings.

Do not make it appear that the society pays a monthly fee and an undefined extra commission on every resident booking.

Industry providers themselves note that society-platform pricing depends on community size, modules, integrations and support requirements. Dedicated onboarding, security-team training and operational support also create real delivery costs, so a single flat price for every society would not be sustainable.

1. Who should receive this package?

The Basic Society Partner package should be offered only after a society has demonstrated:

Successful pilot

↓

Resident demand

↓

Stable sitter supply

↓

Repeat-booking interest

↓

Working gate-access process

↓

Manageable complaint volume

Minimum qualification criteria

Recommended requirements:

At least 10 completed pilot services

At least 10–20 registered pet-parent households

Resident rating of approximately 4.5 or higher

No unresolved critical incident

Working security-entry procedure

At least two eligible local sitters

Committee or facility-manager approval

Evidence of repeat-booking demand

A signed society agreement without active resident demand should not automatically qualify for a paid monthly package.

2. Correct package structure

### Table 13

| Feature | Final treatment |
| --- | --- |
| Society-specific landing page | Included |
| Resident benefit or discount | Included with funding limits |
| Priority sitter matching | Included, subject to eligibility and availability |
| Monthly pet-care report | Included as aggregate reporting |
| Emergency support contact | Included as coordination support |
| Dedicated WhatsApp support | Included with defined hours and SLA |
| Society access roster | Add |
| Society operations contact | Add |
| Incident escalation workflow | Add |
| Quarterly resident awareness activity | Conditional by tier |
| Custom software development | Not included |
| Guaranteed sitter availability | Not included |
| Unlimited 24/7 support | Not included |
| Individual resident data report | Not included |

4. Society-specific landing page

Decision: Approve

The page should be more than a generic marketing page with the society name inserted.

Recommended structure

Society name and approved co-branding

↓

Available services

↓

Resident benefit

↓

Approved-access sitter profiles

↓

How booking works

↓

Trust and safety controls

↓

Emergency coordination explanation

↓

Pricing and policies

↓

Book service CTA

Suggested page sections

Hero section

Trusted pet-care support for Green Meadows residents

Book verified dog walkers and pet sitters with service updates, Report Cards and responsive support.

Society-specific information

Active services

Supported service hours

Important wording

Do not say:

“Guaranteed sitter available.”

Use:

Priority matching with eligible local sitters, subject to availability and care requirements.

Marketing claims should remain legal, honest and truthful, including across websites, SMS, emails, posters and promotional material.

5. Resident discount

Decision: Approve with limits

A resident discount should have:

Clear funding source

Defined validity

Usage limit

Eligible services

Non-transferability rule

Minimum booking value where applicable

Exclusion from other offers where necessary

Recommended resident benefits

Option A — First-booking benefit

₹100 off the first eligible service

Option B — Society package pricing

5 dog walks at a society-specific rate

10 dog walks at a society-specific rate

Option C — Priority benefit

Free first meet-and-greet

or

Priority same-sitter request

A non-price benefit can protect margins better than permanent discounting.

Recommended rule

Do not provide an unlimited permanent percentage discount.

Instead:

First service offer

↓

Five-walk starter plan

↓

Ten-walk or monthly plan

↓

Loyalty benefit based on actual repeat use

Discount transparency

Resident checkout should show:

Standard service price: ₹199

Society partner benefit: −₹30

Final payable amount: ₹169

Do not inflate the displayed standard price merely to create an artificial discount.

6. Priority sitter matching

Decision: Approve with precise definition

Priority matching must mean:

PetSaathi reviews the request before ordinary non-priority requests and searches the approved local sitter pool using service eligibility, Pet Profile controls, availability, travel time and reliability.

It must not mean:

Guaranteed sitter

Guaranteed preferred sitter

Automatic highest-rated sitter

Safety checks bypassed

Immediate booking confirmation

Unlimited replacement

Correct priority-matching order

Service permission

↓

Pet and handling compatibility

↓

Safety status

↓

Availability

↓

Travel feasibility

↓

Society access status

↓

Workload

↓

Reliability ranking

↓

Customer preference

Suggested priority SLA

### Table 14

| Booking type | Initial matching target |
| --- | --- |
| Future standard request | Within 2 business hours |
| Next-day request | Within 30–60 minutes |
| Same-day request | Within 15–30 minutes |
| Active replacement | Immediate operational review |

These should be service targets, not absolute contractual guarantees unless PetSaathi has sufficient supply.

Capacity rule

Priority access should remain subject to:

Active sitter capacity

Daily booking cap

Service area

Society access approval

7. Monthly pet-care report

Decision: Approve as aggregate reporting

The society report should help the committee understand programme performance without exposing private resident information.

Recommended report sections

Programme activity

Registered pet-parent households

Booking requests

Where contractually appropriate:

Information not to include

Resident names

Phone numbers

Bite histories

Individual risk classifications

Emergency contacts

Individual GPS routes

Private media

Payment credentials

The DPDP Act recognises individuals’ interest in protecting their digital personal data, and the final DPDP Rules were published in November 2025 with staged commencement provisions. Society reporting should therefore follow data minimisation, purpose limitation and controlled access.

8. Emergency support contact

Decision: Approve, but describe it as coordination

Use:

PetSaathi emergency escalation and veterinary coordination contact

Do not use:

“PetSaathi emergency vet service”

unless PetSaathi actually employs or contracts qualified veterinary professionals for the advertised service.

Included support

PetSaathi may:

Receive an urgent service report

Contact the pet owner

Contact the saved emergency person

Contact the listed veterinary clinic

Coordinate an approved transport option

Create an incident ticket

Help share authorised pet information

Keep the society security team informed where necessary

Not included

PetSaathi does not:

Diagnose animals

Prescribe medication

Change medication dosage

Guarantee clinic availability

Guarantee ambulance availability

Make owner financial decisions without authority

Emergency availability

Separate:

Active-service emergency support

Available during an active PetSaathi service

General resident veterinary coordination

Available during published support hours

or through approved partner arrangements

Do not imply continuous 24/7 support unless staffing and partners genuinely support it.

9. Dedicated WhatsApp support

Decision: Approve with defined boundaries

“Dedicated” should mean a society-specific support entry point or routing code—not necessarily one employee permanently assigned to one society.

Recommended model

Society WhatsApp number

↓

Resident selects society or booking

↓

Request categorised

↓

↓

SLA and owner assigned

| Category | Suggested response target |
| --- | --- |
| Active-service emergency | Immediate |
| Active-service problem | Under 5–10 minutes |
| Booking question | Under 30 minutes |
| Payment question | Under 1 hour |
| Refund/cancellation | Same working day |
| General enquiry | Same working day |

WhatsApp support should handle

Registration help

It should not become

An unstructured incident database

A veterinary-advice service

NoBrokerHood publicly describes society onboarding, staff training, resident-query handling and an assigned operational person as part of its support model, which illustrates why dedicated society support must be priced and capacity-managed rather than offered without limits.

10. Missing feature: society access roster

Add this to the package.

The committee or security team should have access to a limited active roster containing:

Sitter display name

Photograph

PetSaathi sitter ID

Permitted service

Access status

Validity period

Emergency PetSaathi contact

Do not provide:

Full KYC documents

Personal address

Bank information

Other customer assignments

Private verification notes

Detailed reliability history

Access states

PENDING_REGISTRATION

ACTIVE

TEMPORARILY_PAUSED

EXPIRED

REVOKED

When a sitter is removed or replaced, access status should update promptly.

11. Missing feature: society operations owner

Each paid society should have:

Primary PetSaathi operations contact

Backup operations contact

Society committee contact

Facility/security contact

Emergency escalation contact

This is necessary because monthly fees should purchase operational accountability—not only a landing page.

12. Correct commercial model

Recommended model: retainer plus resident booking margin

Society pays

₹5,000–₹15,000 monthly partnership fee

plus applicable taxes

Residents pay

Published dog-walking, sitting or boarding prices

less any stated society benefit

PetSaathi earns

Monthly society fee

+

Normal service contribution from resident bookings

This is clearer than saying “plus commission.”

What should “commission” mean?

There are three possible meanings.

Model 1 — PetSaathi platform margin

PetSaathi collects the customer price, pays the sitter and retains a platform margin.

Example structure:

Resident service payment

− sitter payout

− payment/support cost

= PetSaathi contribution

This should be called:

Platform service margin

not “commission charged to the society.”

Model 2 — Society receives referral share

PetSaathi pays the society a percentage or fixed amount for eligible bookings.

This creates additional complexity around:

Committee approval

Accounting

Resident-price transparency

Conflict of interest

Refund adjustments

GST and invoicing

Disclosure of incentives

This model is not recommended for the Basic package.

Model 3 — Society pays transaction commission

The society pays PetSaathi an additional amount for each booking.

This should generally be avoided because the society is already paying a monthly fee while residents pay for services.

13. Recommended booking economics

Use a contribution formula:

Monthly society contribution =

Monthly partnership fee

+

Contribution from completed resident bookings

− Resident discounts funded by PetSaathi

− Dedicated support cost

− Society onboarding allocation

− Partner or campaign cost

− Refund and recovery cost

Track separately:

Gross merchandise value

PetSaathi service revenue

Sitter payouts

Payment costs

Partnership-fee revenue

Direct support cost

Contribution margin

Do not call total resident booking value “PetSaathi revenue.”

14. GST and invoicing

The contract should state whether quoted prices are:

Inclusive of applicable taxes

or:

Exclusive of applicable taxes

A registered supplier’s tax invoice must include relevant supplier and recipient details, the service description, total and taxable value, applicable tax rate and tax charged.

Recommended invoice separation:

Society invoice

Basic Society Partnership Fee — July 2026

Resident invoice

Dog Walking Service — Booking BK-1001

Do not combine society subscription fees and individual resident services into an unclear invoice.

The exact GST classification, place-of-supply treatment, registration position and any withholding implications should be confirmed with a chartered accountant before launch.

15. Consumer-facing transparency

Resident checkout and the landing page should clearly display:

Provider identity

Service description

Full price

Discount

Taxes where applicable

Payment method

Cancellation policy

Refund policy

Complaint contact

Expected service timing

Whether the service is delivered by PetSaathi or a partner

The Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules impose duties around providing information relating to returns, refunds, exchanges, payments, grievance redressal and other transaction terms.

16. Recommended contract term

Initial paid term

Three months

This gives enough time to measure:

Repeat plans

Sitter performance

Support workload

Resident conversion

Society complaint patterns

Contribution margin

Renewal options

Month-to-month

or

Six-month renewal

or

Annual partnership with discount

Avoid locking the first partner societies into long annual agreements before the service model is proven.

Recommended notice

30-day termination notice

Immediate suspension should be allowed for:

Safety obstruction

Serious data misuse

Non-payment

Unauthorised branding

Repeated access refusal

Illegal or discriminatory service demands

The package should contain a written service-level schedule.

### Table 16

| Area | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Landing-page availability | Reasonable production uptime target |
| Booking-support response | Under 30 minutes in support hours |
| Active-service issue | Under 5–10 minutes |
| P1 operational alert | Immediate acknowledgement |
| Monthly report | By an agreed monthly date |
| Sitter-access update | Same working day |
| Refund-status response | Same working day |
| Critical incident escalation | Immediate |

Clearly distinguish:

Response time

Mitigation time

A support response does not mean the underlying issue has been resolved.

A society should remain on the paid package when it shows meaningful utilisation.

| Metric | Healthy result |

| Average rating | 4.5+ |
| Repeat customers | Increasing |
| Security-entry failures | Below 3–5% |
| Unresolved critical complaints | 0 |
| Society continuation interest | Positive |
| Contribution after support costs | Positive or improving |

Low-usage response

When a society has fewer than five monthly bookings:

Review resident awareness

↓

Review pricing and service timing

↓

Run one activation campaign

↓

Measure for another month

↓

Downgrade, pause or terminate

Do not keep delivering dedicated support indefinitely to an inactive society.

19. What is wrong in the original package?

Problem 1 — Same price range without tiers

A small 120-home building and a 1,500-home multi-tower community cannot receive the same support for the same operational cost.

Problem 2 — “Commission” is undefined

It is unclear whether the society, resident, sitter or PetSaathi pays it.

Correction
