18–22 cumulative societies contacted

Day 19 — Society meetings and demand validation

Goal

Conduct meetings and secure permission to test resident interest.

The preferred next step after a positive meeting is usually:

A short resident opt-in survey

This is less risky for the society than immediately signing a full agreement.

Survey questions

Pet type

Interested service

Preferred days

Preferred time

Current care arrangement

Interest in first paid trial

Consent to receive PetSaathi information

Do not collect medical details at this stage.

Day 20 — Final outreach batch and proposals

Goal

Proposal-ready societies should have

+

+

+

+

Day 21 — Weekly review

Goal

Analyse quality, not only outreach volume.

Review:

Which channel produced the most replies?

5. Meeting preparation

Every meeting should have a one-page internal briefing.

Society briefing

Society:

Area:

Estimated homes:

Likely pet families:

Contact:

Contact role:

Lead source:

Nearby active sitters:

Likely hero service:

Known access process:

Known concerns:

Recommended pilot:

Desired meeting outcome:

Materials to prepare

Bring or send only the necessary material:

Society pitch deck

One-page pilot summary

Sample Pet Report Card

Sitter-verification explanation

Resident survey link

Pilot pricing summary

Do not begin the meeting by reading the entire pitch deck.

The meeting should be a discovery conversation, not a lecture.

6. Detailed 30-minute meeting agenda

Minutes 0–3 — Introduction

Objective

Establish credibility and explain why PetSaathi selected the society.

Recommended introduction

“Thank you for meeting with us. PetSaathi is developing a structured pet-care programme for apartment communities. We help residents access screened dog walkers and pet sitters while supporting sitter access, service monitoring, Report Cards and formal issue escalation.”

Confirm attendees

Identify:

Decision-maker

Operational stakeholder

Resident representative

Person responsible for the next step

Avoid

Do not spend three minutes describing every future PetSaathi feature.

Minutes 3–7 — Understand the society’s current situation

Ask first; do not pitch immediately

Recommended questions:

Approximately how many homes are occupied?

Is there an active pet-parent group?

How do residents currently find dog walkers or sitters?

How are external helpers registered with security?

Are there recurring pet-care complaints?

Which service timings create the most difficulty?

Do residents frequently request travel-time care?

Has the society previously conducted pet events?

Goal

Identify the real problem.

The society may care more about:

Security access

Dog-walking reliability

Cleanliness

Last-minute replacements

Resident communication

Event coordination

Do not assume every society has the same priority.

Minutes 7–12 — Present the PetSaathi solution

Connect the solution directly to the problems they mentioned.

Example

When the problem is unknown helpers:

“PetSaathi maintains an approved-access sitter roster with a current photo, PetSaathi ID, service permission and access status.”

When the problem is recurring dog walks:

“We can configure regular walking windows with preferred-sitter continuity and backup options.”

When the problem is complaints:

“Every material complaint or safety issue becomes a structured record with an owner, status and resolution.”

Show the operating flow

Resident registers

→ Creates Pet Profile

→ Requests service

→ Eligible sitter assigned

→ Payment verified

→ Gate access prepared

→ Service completed

→ Report Card delivered

Minutes 12–17 — Explain the pilot

Recommended pilot

Duration: 21–30 days

Society setup fee: ₹0

Society partnership fee: ₹0

Residents pay for services booked

Initial services: Dog walking and pet sitting

Booking cap: Based on capacity

Automatic renewal: No

Included

Society landing page

Resident trial offer

Sitter access roster

WhatsApp booking assistance

Pet-parent announcement

Basic awareness material

Aggregate pilot report

Pilot success metrics

Registered pet parents

Paid bookings

Service completion

Punctuality

Report Card delivery

Ratings

Repeat interest

Access problems

Complaints

Important clarity

“No-fee pilot” means there is no society partnership fee. It does not mean all resident pet-care services are free.

Claims about pricing, verification and service capability should remain accurate and supportable. ASCI’s self-regulatory code requires advertising to be legal, honest and truthful.

Minutes 17–22 — Safety and trust process

Explain layered controls

Sitter controls

Identity review

Interview

Service-specific training

Society-access registration

Reliability history

Active safety restrictions

Pet controls

Pet identity

Handling behaviour

Medical and food instructions

Bite or escape history

Emergency contacts

Service-specific controls

Service controls

Arrival update

Start and end evidence

Basic GPS proof for walks

Photo/video updates where required

Pet Report Card

Incident escalation

Recommended safety statement

“PetSaathi does not claim zero risk. We reduce risk through structured information, sitter eligibility, controlled matching, service evidence and documented escalation.”

Veterinary boundary

“Emergency support means coordination with the owner, registered veterinary partners and available transport support. PetSaathi does not diagnose or provide veterinary treatment.”

Minutes 22–27 — Pricing and partnership path

Do not show every detailed pricing variation unless the society asks.

Present the progression

### Table 74

| Stage | Fee |
| --- | --- |
| No-Fee Pilot | ₹0 society fee |
| Basic Society Partner | ₹5,000–₹15,000/month |
| Premium Pet-Care Desk | ₹15,000–₹50,000/month |
| Event/Camp Model | Event-specific pricing |

Explain that pricing depends on:

Number of homes

Active pet families

Towers and gates

Support hours

Coordinator requirements

Booking volume

Event frequency

Reporting needs

Correct commercial statement

“Residents pay separately for the services they book. The society fee covers programme setup, coordination, support and reporting according to the selected package.”

Minutes 27–30 — Decision and next step

Do not end with:

“Please let us know.”

Agree on one specific next action.

Preferred next actions

Society approves resident-interest survey

PetSaathi sends pilot proposal

Security-access review is scheduled

Committee presentation is arranged

Pet-parent introduction is provided

Follow-up date is confirmed

Close with a commitment

“We will send the one-page pilot summary and resident survey by 5:00 PM today. Could we review the survey response together next Tuesday at 3:00 PM?”

Record the agreed date before ending the meeting.

7. Meeting qualification questions

Resident demand

How many residents have asked for pet-care services?

Is there an active pet-parent group?

Is dog walking the main need?

Are travel and festival care common problems?

Would residents pay for a discounted first trial?

Operations

How are external service providers approved?

Which gate should sitters use?

Is advance visitor approval required?

Are replacement sitters allowed?

Are there service-hour restrictions?

Who coordinates security changes?

Decision process

Who approves the pilot?

Is a committee vote required?

Does the facility manager have implementation authority?

What documentation is required?

When is the next committee meeting?

Commercial fit

Does the society prefer no-fee resident-paid services?

Is the society considering a managed amenity?

Is there interest in grooming or vaccination camps?

Would the society sponsor resident benefits?

8. Meeting outcome classifications

After every meeting, choose one outcome.

A — Pilot ready

Requirements:

Decision-maker supportive

Demand likely

Access feasible

Sitter capacity available

Next step:

Send customised pilot proposal

B — Resident demand required

Society is interested but needs evidence.

Next step:

Launch resident opt-in survey

C — Committee review required

The contact supports the proposal but lacks authority.

Next step:

Schedule formal committee presentation

D — Operational review required

Demand exists, but access or sitter capacity is unclear.

Next step:

Facility/security workshop

E — Nurture

Timing is not suitable.

Next step:

Record future review date

F — Disqualified

Examples:

No local sitter supply

No resident demand

Unoccupied property

Outside operating area

Society demands unlawful or unsafe practices

No valid decision path

9. Post-meeting process

Within two hours

Update the CRM:

Attendees

Problems identified

Demand estimate

Current provider

Pilot interest

Access requirements

Objections

Decision process

Agreed next action

Follow-up date

Same working day

Send:

Thank-you message

Meeting summary

Relevant deck or one-page summary

Resident survey or proposal

Agreed next date

Follow-up summary structure

Current challenges

PetSaathi proposal

Pilot scope

Society responsibilities

PetSaathi responsibilities

Information required

Agreed next step

Target date

Do not send the generic agreement unless the society has reached the proposal or committee-review stage.

10. Pet-parent group follow-up

Pet-parent groups are used to validate demand, not to bypass the society committee.

Correct process

Society/group admin gives permission

↓

One approved group announcement

↓

Interested residents reply or open opt-in link

↓

PetSaathi contacts only opted-in residents

↓

Demand summary shared with society

Meta requires businesses to obtain opt-in before sending proactive WhatsApp messages. The opt-in should identify the business and the messaging purpose, and user opt-out requests must be respected.

Do not:

Add residents to WhatsApp groups automatically

Import society contact lists

Message every group member privately

Combine service notifications with unrelated marketing consent

Continue contacting residents after they opt out

11. Outreach channel rules

WhatsApp

Use for:

Warm introductions

Resident-initiated conversations

Meeting confirmation

Approved follow-ups

Survey and pilot links

For WhatsApp Business Platform outreach, proactive conversations require appropriate opt-in and approved message templates.

LinkedIn

Use personalised messages and limit invitations to relevant professional contacts. LinkedIn states that high invitation volume or prohibited automation can result in temporary account restrictions.

Email

Use business email when publicly listed or provided through an introduction.

Include:

Sender identity

Reason for contact

Society relevance

One clear CTA

Easy opt-out instruction

Personal-data handling

CRM records should contain only the information necessary for outreach and partnership management. The final DPDP Rules, 2025 were published on November 14, 2025, with staged implementation provisions; PetSaathi should record the purpose and source of personal contact information, restrict access and honour applicable correction or deletion processes.

12. Common society objections

“We already have local dog walkers.”

Response

“PetSaathi does not need to replace every existing arrangement. The pilot can focus on residents who need verified backup, travel-time sitting, structured updates or recurring plans.”

“Residents will not pay.”

Response

“We recommend validating that through an opt-in resident survey before launching. The society does not need to pay during the initial pilot.”

“We do not want responsibility for incidents.”

Response

“PetSaathi manages individual bookings, sitter assignments, service records and incident workflows. The pilot agreement clearly separates society access coordination from individual pet-care responsibility.”

“How can you guarantee safety?”

Response

“We do not promise zero risk. We use identity review, training, Pet Profiles, controlled matching, service evidence and incident escalation to reduce and manage risk.”

“We cannot share resident data.”

Response

“That is correct. The society can share a QR code or announcement. Interested residents register voluntarily with PetSaathi.”

“Can you guarantee the same sitter?”

Response

“We support preferred-sitter continuity, but confirmation depends on availability, eligibility and service conditions. A replacement process is available when required.”

“The price is too high.”

Response

“The initial pilot has no society partnership fee. After the pilot, the package is selected according to actual resident demand and support requirements.”

15. Common Week 3 mistakes

Mistake 1 — Sending 30 generic messages

Correction: Contact fewer societies with stronger personalisation and channel selection.

Mistake 2 — Pitching before understanding the problem

Correction: Spend the first seven minutes asking questions.

Mistake 3 — Using all 10 slides in every meeting

Correction: Show only the slides relevant to that society’s concerns.

Mistake 4 — Leaving without a scheduled next action

Correction: Agree on a survey, proposal, committee meeting or review date.

Mistake 5 — Sending the pilot agreement too early

Correction: Send a one-page summary first; provide the agreement after genuine interest.

Mistake 6 — Treating verbal interest as a qualified opportunity

Correction: Record the approval process, decision-maker and timeline.

Mistake 7 — Promising safety or sitter availability

Correction: Explain controls, eligibility and capacity honestly.

Mistake 8 — Ignoring pet-parent demand

Correction: Validate whether residents will actually book and pay.

Mistake 9 — Failing to follow up quickly

Correction: Send a written summary and next step on the same working day.

Week 3 definition of done

Week 3 is complete only when:

Outreach

Thirty qualified societies have received personalised contact.

Outreach sources and permissions are recorded.

Warm introductions are prioritised.

WhatsApp outreach follows opt-in requirements.

Declines and opt-outs are respected.

Meetings

Five to eight discovery meetings are scheduled or completed.

Every meeting follows a structured agenda.

Society problems are documented before pitching.

Decision-makers and approval processes are identified.

Every meeting ends with one specific next action.

Qualification

Resident demand is estimated.

Sitter capacity is checked.

Gate and security feasibility are understood.

The correct pilot service is selected.

Weak or unsuitable societies are moved to nurture or lost.

CRM

Every interaction is logged.

Every active opportunity has an owner.

Every active opportunity has a next-action date.

Objections and decision timelines are recorded.

Duplicate contact attempts are prevented.

Commercial progress

Three to five societies approve a resident survey.

Two to four societies request a pilot summary or proposal.

No unsupported promise is made to secure a meeting.

Sales materials remain consistent with actual operations.

Simple explanation for professor

“During Week 3, PetSaathi will contact 30 qualified apartment societies and aim to schedule five to eight meetings.

Every morning, we will review the CRM and identify important follow-ups. From 10:00 AM to 12:00 PM, we will contact society managers, RWA members and resident representatives using personalised calls, emails, LinkedIn messages or permission-based WhatsApp messages.

Each society meeting will last approximately 30 minutes. First, we will introduce PetSaathi and ask about the society’s current pet-care and security problems. We will then explain our solution, including verified sitter access, Pet Profiles, service updates, Report Cards and incident handling.

After understanding the society’s needs, we will present the no-fee pilot. The society will not pay a setup fee during the pilot, while residents will pay only for the services they book.

We will also explain the safety system, pricing and long-term packages. Every meeting must finish with one specific next step, such as a resident survey, pilot proposal, committee presentation or access review.

After the meeting, the team will update the CRM and send a written summary on the same day. Week 3 will be successful when PetSaathi has five to eight serious society discussions, three to five resident-demand surveys and two to four potential pilot opportunities.”

PetSaathi Phase 7 — Week 4: First Society Pilots 🏢🐾🚀

Week 4 objective

Convert the strongest Week 3 opportunities into 2–3 operational society pilots that produce real paid services—not only signed agreements or resident registrations.

The complete Week 4 outcome should be:

2–3 signed pilot societies

↓

Society access and support configured

↓

50–100 residents opt in

↓

20–50 paid services completed or scheduled

↓

Report Cards and reviews collected

↓

Repeat demand measured

↓

Society continuation decision prepared

The strongest approach is to activate societies in the same geographic cluster so they can share sitter capacity, backup caregivers, veterinary partners and operational support.

1. Correct interpretation of the Week 4 targets

### Table 76

| Metric | Proposed target | Final definition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pilot societies | 2–3 | Agreement signed and operational setup completed |
| Pet-parent leads | 50–100 | Residents who voluntarily opt in and identify a service need |
| Paid bookings | 20–50 | Payment verified and booking accepted into the service workflow |
| Repeat interest | 10+ | Residents requesting another service or package details |
| Society feedback | Collected | Structured committee, security and resident feedback |

Important distinction

A society should not count as an active pilot merely because its committee verbally agrees.

An active pilot requires:

Signed pilot document

+ authorised contacts

+ gate-access process

+ resident communication approval

+ usable sitter capacity

+ active booking page

Similarly, a WhatsApp-group member or poster viewer is not automatically a qualified lead. Residents should register voluntarily through an opt-in page or resident-initiated WhatsApp interaction. Meta requires businesses to obtain opt-in before proactively messaging people on WhatsApp.

2. Week 4 readiness gate

Before launching any society, verify six areas.

Society readiness

Pilot agreement signed

Authorised committee or facility contact

Security contact confirmed

Approved communication channel

Service timings confirmed

Access and replacement procedures documented

Product readiness

Society landing page works

Resident verification works

Pet Profile form works

Booking and payment work

Report Card workflow works

Support and incident actions work

Supply readiness

Primary walkers available

Pet sitters available

Backup candidates available

Peak-slot capacity calculated

No sitter has an active safety restriction

Commercial readiness

Taxes and invoices configured where applicable

Daily booking cap defined

Privacy readiness

Resident notice is visible

Society receives only aggregate reporting

Unit numbers and Pet Profiles are restricted

WhatsApp communication is opt-in

Media and Report Cards are private

India’s final Digital Personal Data Protection Rules were published on November 14, 2025, with different provisions commencing on staged timelines. The pilot should therefore use clear notices, limited collection and controlled access from the beginning.

Operational readiness

Named PetSaathi pilot owner

Backup operations owner

Emergency escalation contact

Daily dashboard

Society-specific FAQ

Stop/pause procedure

3. End-to-end pilot launch process

Stage 1 — Society agrees

Confirm the parties

Record:

Society legal/operating name

Society responsibilities

PetSaathi responsibilities

Recommended pilot period

21–30 days

Week 4 launches the pilot; the pilot itself may continue into subsequent weeks.

The agreement should state

No society setup or partnership fee during the pilot

Residents pay for individual services

No automatic renewal

No guarantee of sitter availability

PetSaathi controls assignments

Either party may pause the pilot for safety or operational reasons

This is not yet ACTIVE.

Stage 2 — Create the society landing page

The page becomes the main resident entry point.

Pilot-page indexing rule

A newly created pilot page with limited unique information can initially use:

noindex, follow

Make it indexable only when the partnership is active and the page has meaningful, accurate local content. Google classifies mass-produced pages with little unique user value as potential scaled-content or doorway abuse.

Technical checks

Analytics events fire correctly

Public API excludes private fields

Digital service pages should clearly disclose service information, pricing, payment, refunds and grievance mechanisms. The Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules apply to goods and services transacted through digital platforms.

7. Create and confirm the first bookings

Booking workflow

Resident chooses service

↓

Selects or creates Pet Profile

↓

Chooses date/time

↓

Confirms society/unit

↓

Server calculates price

↓

Booking request created

↓

PetSaathi reviews requirements

↓

Eligible sitter assigned

↓

Resident pays

↓

Payment verified

↓

Booking confirmed

Payment rule

Do not confirm a prepaid pilot booking based only on the frontend success screen.

Razorpay describes server-side payment-signature verification as mandatory and recommends webhooks or API verification to handle callback failures and confirm payment state.

Required confirmation guard

Payment signature verified

+ payment captured/confirmed

+ active sitter assignment

+ no unresolved safety block

= CONFIRMED

Razorpay webhooks should also have their signatures validated before processing.

Do not ask only:

Pet-parent residents

Was the service on time?

Would you book again?

What prevented you from booking?

16. Pilot booking caps

Do not open unlimited capacity.

Recommended initial cap

### Table 84

| Stage | Maximum |
| --- | --- |
| First service day | 3–5 bookings |
| After first review | 5–10/day |
| Stable operation | Increase based on sitter capacity |

Pause new bookings when

No backup coverage exists

Entry failures repeat

Report Cards are overdue

Sitter utilisation is unsafe

Active incident is unresolved

Payment reconciliation fails

Support queue exceeds capacity

Use:

AVAILABLE

LIMITED_AVAILABILITY

WAITLIST

PAUSED

on the society page.

17. Pilot decision states

At the end of the initial launch period, classify each society.

CONTINUE_PILOT

Use when:

Demand exists

Sample is still small

Operations are improving

No critical blocker exists

CONVERT_TO_BASIC_PARTNER

Use when:

Consistent paid demand

Stable sitter access

Repeat interest

Society wants ongoing reporting/support

OFFER_PREMIUM_DESK

Use when:

High resident density

Many recurring slots

Complex access needs

Coordinator demand exists

EVENT_FIRST_STRATEGY

Use when:

Resident awareness is low

Society wants a grooming, veterinary or training event first

Recurring service demand is uncertain

PAUSE_AND_FIX

Use when:

Entry process fails

Sitter supply is insufficient

Support load is too high

Product defects affect bookings

TERMINATE_PILOT

Use when:

Society obstructs safety processes

Resident demand is absent

Data is misused

Unlawful pet-control expectations persist

Economics are structurally unsustainable

18. Common Week 4 mistakes

Mistake 1 — Calling a signed agreement a successful pilot

Correction: A successful pilot requires registrations, paid services and operational evidence.

Mistake 2 — Activating all societies on the same day

Correction: Stagger launches by 24–48 hours where possible.

Mistake 3 — Promoting before sitter supply is ready

Correction: Confirm slot-level capacity before announcements.

Mistake 4 — Collecting leads without consent

Correction: Use resident opt-in forms and resident-initiated WhatsApp conversations.

Mistake 5 — Counting unpaid booking requests as paid bookings

Correction: Count only payments verified by the backend.

Mistake 6 — Offering unlimited ₹99 bookings

Correction: Limit the offer by household, dates and capacity.

Mistake 7 — Sending the society private resident data

Correction: Provide aggregate performance only.

Mistake 8 — Measuring “repeat interest” only by survey answers

Correction: Track second requests, payments and completed services separately.

Mistake 9 — Waiting until the end to ask for feedback

Correction: Gather feedback after the first 5–10 services.

Mistake 10 — Continuing despite operational warning signs

Correction: Use booking caps, waitlists and pause controls.

Week 4 definition of done

Week 4 is complete only when:

Partnership

Two or three authorised pilot agreements are signed.

Pilot dates and scope are clear.

Each society has operational contacts.

Society responsibilities are documented.

Residents

Registration is voluntary.

Verification works.

Leads are qualified.

Pet Profiles are private.

Opt-outs are respected.

Sitter operations

Local sitter capacity exists.

Security access is active.

Backup candidates are identified.

Booking caps reflect actual capacity.

No single sitter creates unsafe dependency.

Payments and bookings

Pricing is server-controlled.

Payments are verified.

Failed payments do not confirm bookings.

Cancellations and refunds are traceable.

Every status transition is audited.

Services

First services complete successfully.

Entry/start/end events are recorded.

Report Cards are delivered.

Reviews and concerns are routed correctly.

Critical incidents have immediate ownership.

Business validation

20–50 paid bookings are generated or firmly confirmed.

At least ten residents show repeat intent.

Stronger repeat signals are tracked separately.

Society and security feedback is collected.

Each society receives a continuation recommendation.

Simple explanation for professor

“During Week 4, PetSaathi will launch its first two or three apartment-society pilots.

First, each society will sign a pilot agreement explaining the services, dates, payment model, sitter-access process, resident privacy and responsibilities of both parties.

PetSaathi will then create a society-specific landing page showing the trial offer, available services, local sitter information, booking steps and support contact.

The society will share an approved announcement and QR code with residents. Interested pet parents will register voluntarily rather than the society giving PetSaathi its full contact database.

After registration, residents will create their Pet Profiles. PetSaathi will analyse demand and map nearby walkers, pet sitters and backup caregivers. Only the number of booking slots that can be safely supported will be opened.

Residents can then book the introductory service. Payment will be verified through the backend before the booking becomes confirmed. The assigned sitter will complete the service and submit a Pet Report Card containing timings, care updates, media and any concerns.

After every service, the resident can submit a review and request the same sitter or a walking package. PetSaathi will separately track repeat interest, second booking requests and actual second paid services.

At the end of the week, PetSaathi will collect feedback from residents, society management and security. Each society will then be classified as ready to continue, ready for a paid partnership, needing operational improvements or unsuitable for further launch.

The Week 4 success condition is not only signing two or three societies. It is completing real paid services safely and proving that residents want to book again.”

PetSaathi Phase 7 — Week 5: Scale Pilots and Run Society Events 🏢🐾

Week 5 objective

Week 5 moves PetSaathi from a small pilot experiment into a controlled society cluster.

The goal is to:

Stabilise the first 2–3 pilots

↓

Add 3–5 nearby societies

↓

Run 1–3 carefully controlled events

↓

Convert residents into paid services

↓

Build a verified local partner network

↓

Collect credible testimonials

↓

Identify the strongest societies and services

By the end of Week 5, PetSaathi should have 5–8 total pilot societies, but they should ideally remain within the same two or three nearby localities.

Do not spread eight societies across an entire city. Density is still more valuable than geographic reach.

1. Correct Week 5 targets

### Table 85

| Metric | Proposed target | Correct measurement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Total pilot societies | 5–8 | Signed, configured and operational |
| Society bookings | 75–150 | Paid bookings, tracked separately from completed services |
| Events or camps | 1–3 | Properly approved and operationally completed |
| Veterinary/grooming partners | 3–5 | Verified, contracted or pilot-approved partners |
| Testimonials | 5–10 | Real, consented and attributable feedback |

Add these quality targets

### Table 86

| Metric | Recommended target |
| --- | --- |
| Completed booking rate | 90%+ |
| On-time service rate | 95%+ |
| Report Card delivery | 98%+ |
| Security-entry success | 97%+ |
| Customer rating | 4.6+ |
| Repeat-interest rate | 25%+ |
| Critical unresolved incidents | 0 |
| Event attendee satisfaction | 4.5+ |
| Partner complaints unresolved | 0 |

2. Important distinction: signed versus active societies

A society should count as an active pilot only when:

Pilot approval signed

+

Society landing page live

+

Resident communication published

+

Local sitter capacity confirmed

+

Security/access workflow working

+

At least one resident registration or booking

Use these statuses:

PROSPECT

PILOT_APPROVED

SETUP_IN_PROGRESS

RESIDENT_REGISTRATION_OPEN

ACTIVE_PILOT

PAUSED

COMPLETED

CONVERTED

A society with a signed agreement but no residents, sitters or bookings should remain SETUP_IN_PROGRESS.

3. Society expansion strategy

Do not add all 3–5 societies at once

Recommended activation sequence:

Review first pilot performance

↓

Select next 2 strongest societies

↓

Launch and monitor

↓

Fix common problems

↓

Activate remaining 1–3 societies

Recommended cluster model

For example, in Ahmedabad:

Cluster A:

Bopal + South Bopal

Cluster B:

Satellite + Prahlad Nagar

Each cluster should ideally share:

Dog walkers

Backup sitters

Operations owner

Emergency veterinary contacts

Grooming partners

Event equipment

Support procedures

Choose the next 3–5 societies based on:

| Factor | Weight |
| --- | --- |
| Confirmed resident demand | 25% |
| Local sitter capacity | 20% |
| Geographic proximity | 15% |
| Committee readiness | 15% |
| Security/access feasibility | 10% |
| Society size and occupancy | 10% |
| Partner-event potential | 5% |

Do not activate a society when

No eligible local sitter is available.

All peak slots are already full.

Security access remains unresolved.

The society expects illegal or discriminatory pet controls.

Resident demand is only assumed.

Existing pilots have unresolved safety failures.

Support staff cannot monitor additional bookings.

5. Week 5 operating model

Week 5 contains two parallel workstreams.

Workstream A — Scale recurring care services

Resident announcement

→ Pet Profile

→ Trial booking

→ Completed service

→ Report Card

→ Review

→ Repeat plan

Workstream B — Run society events

Society and partner approval

→ Resident registration

→ Event delivery

→ Feedback

→ Pet Profile creation

→ Trial service conversion

Events should support the main marketplace. They should not distract the team from dog walking, sitting and repeat service quality.

6. Society event strategy

Each event should have one primary purpose.

### Table 88

| Event | Primary purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Pet health-check camp | Trust and veterinary-partner credibility |
| Grooming day | Direct event revenue and resident activation |
| Dog-walking trial day | Convert residents into recurring services |
| Pet-parent meetup | Community trust and demand discovery |
| Diwali safety session | Seasonal education and early-care bookings |
| Puppy-care workshop | Education and long-term customer acquisition |

Do not run an event merely to produce social-media photographs.

Every event should have:

Target audience

Capacity

Partner

Revenue model

Registration process

Consent

Safety plan

Conversion goal

Success metrics

7. Event 1 — Pet health-check camp

Purpose

Build resident trust and introduce PetSaathi through a useful professional service.

Correct responsibility model

PetSaathi acts as:

Event coordinator

Registration platform

Resident communication layer

Queue and payment coordinator

Follow-up service platform

A registered veterinary practitioner remains responsible for:

Clinical examination

Medical advice

Diagnosis

Prescription

Referral

Clinical records

The Indian Veterinary Council Act regulates veterinary practice and provides for registered veterinary practitioners and veterinary councils. Therefore, PetSaathi should not present ordinary staff or event coordinators as veterinary professionals.

Possible services

General physical check

Weight assessment

Skin or coat observation

Dental observation

Vaccination-record review

Preventive-care guidance

Clinic referral

Recommended workflow

Resident registers

↓

Pet and appointment verified

↓

Owner consent confirmed

↓

Veterinarian examines pet

↓

Clinical summary provided

↓

Follow-up recommendation recorded

↓

PetSaathi offers relevant care services separately

Revenue model

Prefer:

Fixed coordination fee

Fixed fee per completed appointment

Society-sponsored event

Avoid commission linked directly to how many treatments or vaccines are recommended.

Conversion offer

After the appointment:

Complete your PetSaathi Pet Profile and receive a first dog-walking or pet-sitting offer.

8. Event 2 — Grooming day

Purpose

Generate event revenue and attract residents who may later use recurring care services.

Partner requirements

The grooming partner should provide:

Experienced staff

Safe equipment

Hygiene process

Pet-handling process

Service menu

Capacity limits

Incident procedure

Cleaning and waste disposal

Recommended services

Bathing

Brushing

Basic coat care

Nail trimming when appropriate

Basic hygiene services

Do not include as routine grooming services

Sedation

Medical skin treatment

Wound treatment

Veterinary dental procedures

Any service beyond partner competence

Revenue models

Revenue share

Resident pays service price

↓

Refunds/discounts adjusted

↓

Partner share calculated

↓

PetSaathi coordination share calculated

Fixed partner fee

PetSaathi receives an agreed amount per completed appointment.

Society event fee

The society pays PetSaathi to coordinate the event.

Required transparency

Residents should clearly see:

Service provider

Service description

Price

Cancellation policy

Refund policy

Complaint channel

India’s Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules apply to digital transactions for goods and services and require clear consumer-facing information and grievance arrangements.

9. Event 3 — Dog-walking trial day

Purpose

This should be the highest-priority Week 5 event because it directly converts residents into PetSaathi’s strongest recurring service.

Recommended format

Do not gather many unknown dogs for a group walk.

Use individual scheduled trial appointments:

09:00–09:30 — Resident 1

09:45–10:15 — Resident 2

10:30–11:00 — Resident 3

Each trial includes:

Pet Profile review

Owner handover

Equipment check

Individual walk

Basic GPS evidence

Photo update

Pet Report Card

Repeat offer

Safety rule

Two dogs should not be combined merely because they booked the same event.

Shared walking requires:

Compatibility review

Owner consent

Sitter capability

Appropriate equipment

Separate service policy

Recommended offer

30-minute trial walk:

₹99–₹149

Trial-day conversion flow

Trial completed

↓

Report Card delivered

↓

Customer review requested

↓

Same walker shown

↓

Five-walk pack offered

↓

Regular society time slot selected

Success metrics

Trials completed

On-time rate

Customer rating

Same-sitter requests

Five-walk plan purchases

Second completed services

10. Event 4 — Pet-parent meetup

Purpose

Build community trust, understand resident needs and create warm leads.

Suggested agenda

1. Introduction to PetSaathi

2. Responsible society pet-care process

3. How sitter verification works

4. Pet Profile demonstration

5. Report Card demonstration

6. Lost-pet alert explanation

7. Upcoming care services and events

8. Resident questions

Useful discussion topics

Daily walking needs

Holiday care

Boarding concerns

Society access

Emergency contacts

Common-area coordination

Same-sitter preferences

Commercial use

The meetup can promote:

Trial dog walks

Five-walk plans

Pet-sitting bookings

Upcoming camps

Society pet-parent community

Privacy rule

Do not automatically add every attendee to PetSaathi marketing lists or WhatsApp groups.

Residents should opt in for:

Service information

Event reminders

Lost-pet alerts

Marketing offers

Meta requires businesses to obtain opt-in before sending people WhatsApp business messages, and the opt-in should make the business and messaging purpose clear.

11. Event 5 — Diwali pet-safety session

Purpose

Build seasonal trust and generate early bookings for walking, sitting and controlled boarding.

Timing

Run the session before peak Diwali travel and fireworks activity, not after booking capacity is already full.

Suggested topics

Fireworks anxiety preparation

Safe indoor space

Identification tag and current pet photo

Walk timing adjustments

Door and gate safety

Pet Profile health update

Emergency veterinarian details

Lost-pet prevention

Travel and boarding deadlines

Medication discussions with the pet’s veterinarian

Presenter model

Use:

Registered veterinarian for medical guidance

Screened trainer or behaviour professional for behaviour preparation

PetSaathi operations team for booking and safety workflows

PetSaathi staff should not diagnose anxiety or recommend medication.

Conversion opportunities

Diwali pet-sitting bookings

Individual walking slots

Boarding beta waitlist

Same-sitter festival continuity

Emergency-contact verification

Lost-pet alert preparation

12. Event 6 — Puppy-care workshop

Purpose

Acquire long-term customers early in the pet-parent journey.

Suggested topics

Puppy routine

Toilet schedule

Safe socialisation

Leash foundations

Bite inhibition education

Food and water routine

Veterinary schedule awareness

Home safety

Separation preparation

Pet Profile creation

Partner model

Use:

Registered veterinarian for health topics

Humane trainer for training topics

PetSaathi for care-service planning

Safety controls

Avoid uncontrolled puppy mixing.

Use limited capacity.

Require secure carriers or leashes.

Keep nervous puppies separated.

Do not force interactions.

Do not diagnose medical or behavioural conditions publicly.

The Prevention of Cruelty to Animals Act is India’s primary central legislation against unnecessary animal pain or suffering; event design and handling should remain humane and controlled.

The DPDP Rules, 2025 were officially published on November 14, 2025 with staged commencement timelines. PetSaathi should provide a clear notice explaining what data is collected, why it is required, which partner receives it and how long it will be retained.

18. Testimonials

Week 5 target

Collect 5–10 credible testimonials.

Possible sources:

Pet parents

Facility manager

RWA representative

Security supervisor

Veterinary partner

Grooming partner

Testimonial consent

Before publishing, record:

Exact approved wording

Name display preference

Society display permission

Pet photo permission

Channel permission

Expiry or withdrawal process

Display options

Full first name + pet name

First name + initial

Anonymous society resident

Facility manager, society name

Never fabricate or rewrite beyond approval

A real customer statement should not be exaggerated into a guarantee.

For example:

Original:

“The walker was on time and the report was useful.”

Do not transform it into:

“PetSaathi is the safest and best pet-care company in India.”

Advertising claims and endorsements should remain truthful and not misleading.

Testimonial types

Service testimonial

“The sitter arrived on time and Bruno’s Report Card made the service very transparent.”

Society testimonial

“The approved sitter list made the security process easier during the pilot.”

Partner testimonial

“The society registration process helped us organise appointments efficiently.”

19. Event-to-booking conversion

Every event needs a follow-up campaign.

Within two hours

Thank attendees.

Share partner record or service confirmation.

Provide support contact.

Within 24 hours

Request event feedback.

Invite residents to complete Pet Profiles.

Offer trial dog walking or pet sitting.

Within 48–72 hours

Remind interested residents.

Offer same-society service slots.

Promote walking packages.

Conversion funnel

Event registered

↓

Attended

↓

Pet Profile started

↓

Pet Profile completed

↓

Trial booking requested

↓

Payment completed

↓

Service completed

↓

Repeat booking

| Funnel stage | Initial target |
| --- | --- |
| Registered → attended | 60–80% |
| Attended → qualified lead | 60%+ |
| Lead → Pet Profile completed | 30–50% |
| Lead → trial booking | 15–30% |
| Trial → completed service | 90%+ |
| Completed service → repeat | 25–35% |

These are internal planning targets and should be updated from actual pilot data.

GST invoice rules require prescribed information on invoices for taxable services and generally provide a 30-day period for issuing service invoices, subject to applicable exceptions. Final tax treatment should be confirmed with a chartered accountant.

23. Major risks during Week 5

Risk 1 — Scaling before Week 4 is stable

Control

Do not add new societies when:

Report Cards are missing.

Payments are unreconciled.

Existing incidents remain unresolved.

No backup sitters exist.

Support response is deteriorating.

Risk 2 — Events consume all operations capacity

Control

Assign separate owners for:

Normal bookings

Event operations

Partner coordination

Do not cancel regular care services to run promotional events.

Risk 3 — Overcrowded events

Control

Use:

Appointment slots

Capacity limit

Waitlist

Separate pet waiting areas

Secure leashes/carriers

No forced interaction

Risk 4 — Unverified professional partner

Control

Verify identity, credentials, service scope and responsibility before promotion.

Risk 5 — Resident data shared with partners

Control

Partners receive only the data needed to deliver the booked service. Marketing access requires separate resident permission.

Risk 6 — Discount-driven low-quality bookings

Control

Keep services prepaid and limit introductory offers by household and capacity.

Risk 7 — Testimonials without consent

Control

Use a formal testimonial approval process.

24. Common Week 5 mistakes

Mistake 1 — Adding five societies without sitter capacity

Correction: Calculate slot-level capacity before announcements.

Mistake 2 — Running three events on one weekend

Correction: Run one event first, review operations and then expand.

Mistake 3 — Calling every veterinary clinic a partner

Correction: A partner must be reviewed and have an agreed operating scope.

Mistake 4 — Counting event attendees as customers

Correction: Track attendees, leads, paid bookings and repeat customers separately.

Mistake 5 — Using camps only for branding

Correction: Every event needs a conversion path into Pet Profiles and bookings.

Mistake 6 — Combining unfamiliar dogs for convenience

Correction: Use individual appointments unless compatibility is separately approved.

Mistake 7 — Publishing private attendee information

Correction: Society and sponsors receive aggregate results unless residents separately opt in.

Mistake 8 — Collecting generic testimonials

Correction: Collect specific feedback about punctuality, transparency, service quality or society operations.

Areas with insufficient capacity

Week 5 definition of done

Week 5 is complete only when:

Societies

Five to eight pilots are operational.

New societies have real resident demand.

Each has active sitter capacity.

Security workflows function.

Booking caps are enforced.

Bookings

Paid and completed bookings are reported separately.

Payments are verified.

Services are monitored.

Report Cards are delivered.

Repeat demand is tracked.

Events

At least one event is completed safely.

Partner responsibility is documented.

Registration and consent are controlled.

Event attendees are offered a relevant next step.

Financial reconciliation is complete.

Partners

Veterinary partners have appropriate professional verification.

Grooming and training partners have defined service scopes.

Refund and complaint responsibility is clear.

Partner performance is recorded.

Testimonials

Every published testimonial is genuine.

The customer or partner approved the wording.

Personal data and images are used only with permission.

No statement is converted into an unsupported safety claim.

Operations

Existing pilot quality has not fallen.

No critical incident remains unresolved.

Support workload remains manageable.

The team can explain which societies, events and services deserve further investment.

Final Week 5 operating principle

Scale only the society services that are already reliable, and use events to create qualified demand—not to hide weak recurring booking performance.

The most important Week 5 result is not the number of events or society logos.

It is:

More nearby residents

+

More completed paid services

+

Reliable local sitters

+

Useful partners

+

Strong repeat intent

+

No decline in safety or service quality

Simple explanation for professor

“During Week 5, PetSaathi will expand from the first two or three societies to a total of five to eight active pilot societies.

We will add only societies located near the existing pilot areas so that walkers, backup sitters, veterinary partners and the operations team can serve them efficiently.

PetSaathi will also conduct one to three events. A pet health-check camp will be organised through a registered veterinary partner. A grooming day will be delivered through a verified grooming provider. A dog-walking trial day will allow residents to experience an individual paid trial walk and receive a Pet Report Card.

Other event options include pet-parent meetings, Diwali safety sessions and puppy-care workshops. PetSaathi will coordinate registrations and operations, while qualified partners remain responsible for veterinary, grooming or training services.

After every event, residents will be invited to complete their Pet Profiles and book a trial dog walk or pet-sitting service. This means the event is used to create real customers instead of only social-media visibility.

By the end of Week 5, the goal is to have five to eight active pilot societies, 75 to 150 paid society bookings, three to five approved partners and five to ten genuine testimonials.

PetSaathi will continue scaling only if service completion, punctuality, Report Cards, security access and customer satisfaction remain strong.”

PetSaathi Phase 7 — Weeks 6–8: Convert Pilots into Paid Society Partnerships 🏢🐾💼

Executive decision

The conversion model is approved, but a society should not become a paid partner based only on registrations, booking requests or positive verbal feedback.

A successful conversion must prove four things:

Resident demand

+

Reliable service delivery

+

Positive operating economics

+

Society willingness to continue

The recommended Weeks 6–8 sequence is:

Week 6 — Audit pilot performance

↓

Week 7 — Present and negotiate paid proposal

↓

Week 8 — Sign, invoice and activate paid partnership

1. Correct paid-conversion criteria

Your original criteria are a useful minimum:

### Table 98

| Metric | Original target |
| --- | --- |
| Pet-parent leads | 30+ |
| Paid bookings | 15+ |
| Repeat customers | 5+ |
| Average rating | 4.5+ |
| RWA feedback | Positive |
| Complaints | Manageable |

However, several terms need exact definitions.

A. Pet-parent leads: 30+

Approved with modification

Count only qualified opt-in residents who:

Live in or are authorised occupants of the society

Voluntarily registered

Identified a genuine pet-care requirement

Provided valid contact information

Accepted the relevant communication and privacy notice

Have not opted out

Do not count:

WhatsApp-group members

Poster views

Event visitors who did not register

Unverified phone numbers

Residents imported from a society list without a valid process

The DPDP Rules, 2025 were officially published on November 14, 2025 with staged commencement provisions. PetSaathi should therefore maintain clear notices, defined data purposes, limited access and appropriate retention for pilot and partnership data.

Stronger target

Minimum: 30 qualified leads

Strong: 50+

Excellent: 75+

Also report:

Qualified leads

→ Pet Profiles completed

→ First paid booking

B. Paid bookings: 15+

Modify to completed paid bookings

A booking should count toward conversion only when:

Payment verified

+

Sitter assigned

+

Service completed

+

Report Card delivered

A booking request, payment attempt or future confirmed booking is useful pipeline data, but it does not prove service delivery.

Recommended threshold

### Table 99

| Level | Completed paid services |
| --- | --- |
| Minimum conversion evidence | 15 |
| Strong Basic Partner evidence | 25–40 |
| Premium Desk consideration | 50+ with recurring demand |

Also report:

Paid bookings created

Completed bookings

Cancelled bookings

Refunded bookings

No-shows

Services still scheduled

Digital service transactions should clearly present service information, prices, payment terms, cancellation/refund terms and grievance channels. The Consumer Protection (E-Commerce) Rules apply to goods and services transacted over digital or electronic networks.

C. Repeat customers: 5+

Important correction

Do not define repeat customer as:

“The resident said they are interested in booking again.”

Use:

A resident who completed at least one additional paid service after the introductory booking.

Track the stages separately:

REPEAT_INTERESTED

REPEAT_REQUESTED

SECOND_BOOKING_PAID

SECOND_SERVICE_COMPLETED

PACKAGE_PURCHASED

PACKAGE_CREDITS_USED

Recommended threshold

Minimum:

5 second paid bookings or package purchases

Stronger:

25%+ of eligible first-time customers repeat

Best evidence:

Second service completed successfully

Example:

28 first paid customers

7 completed second services

Repeat completion rate:

25%

A package purchase is useful, but PetSaathi should also monitor whether the customer actually consumes the package credits.

D. Rating: 4.5+

Approve with sample-size protection

A 4.8 rating from three residents is not sufficient evidence.

Report:

Average rating

+

Number of submitted reviews

+

Review response rate

+

Rating distribution

Recommended threshold

Average rating: 4.5+

Minimum completed reviews: 10

Review response rate: 40–50%+

No unresolved 1-star safety complaint

Example:

Average rating: 4.7/5

Reviews submitted: 18

Review response rate: 64%

1–2 star reviews: 1

Critical unresolved reviews: 0

Any public conversion claim such as “4.7-rated service” must match the real data and sample. ASCI’s code requires advertising and promotional representations to be legal, honest, truthful and capable of substantiation.

E. RWA feedback: positive

Replace vague feedback with a scorecard

Collect feedback from:

RWA/AOA representative

Facility manager

Security supervisor

Pet-parent residents

Non-pet resident complaints, where relevant

Society scorecard

### Table 100

| Question | Rating |
| --- | --- |
| Was pilot setup clear? | 1–5 |
| Was the sitter-access roster useful? | 1–5 |
| Was PetSaathi responsive? | 1–5 |
| Were complaints handled properly? | 1–5 |
| Did the service provide resident value? | 1–5 |
| Should the society continue? | Yes/No |
| Preferred partnership package | Pilot/Basic/Premium/Event |

Paid-conversion requirement

Authorised society representative supports continuation

+

Security/facility process is workable

+

No major unresolved operational objection

A few residents liking the service does not replace formal society approval.

F. Complaints: manageable

Replace with measurable limits

Recommended thresholds:

### Table 101

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Critical unresolved complaints | 0 |
| Total society complaints | Below 5 per 100 completed services |
| Security-entry failures | Below 3–5% |
| Complaint acknowledgement | Same working day |
| Active-service issue response | Under 5–10 minutes |
| Complaints with assigned owner | 100% |
| Repeated unresolved complaint pattern | 0 |

Complaint categories should include:

Sitter access

Punctuality

Sitter conduct

Pet handling

Cleanliness

Common-area use

Payment/refund

Privacy

Service quality

Safety incident

“Manageable” should never be used to hide one serious unresolved safety incident.

2. Additional mandatory conversion criteria

The original criteria should be expanded with operational and financial gates.

Service reliability

### Table 102

| Metric | Target |
| --- | --- |
| Paid → completed service | 90%+ |
| On-time arrival | 95%+ |
| Report Card delivery | 98%+ |
| Sitter no-show rate | Below 3–5% |
| Security-entry success | 97%+ |
| Payment reconciliation | 100% |
| Unresolved critical incidents | 0 |

Sitter capacity

The society should have:

At least two usable primary caregivers

Genuine backup coverage for priority services

No unsafe dependency on one sitter

Enough capacity for peak walking windows

No unresolved verification or training expiry

Customer retention

Second completed booking rate: 20–25%+

or

5+ recurring/package customers

Operational workload

Measure:

Support minutes per booking

Admin interventions per booking

Replacement frequency

Manual payment corrections

Access failures

Report Card follow-ups

A society generating 20 bookings but requiring three hours of manual support per booking may not be ready for a standard paid package.

3. Unit economics gate

A society should not be converted solely because it has demand. It should have a credible path to positive contribution.

Track four separate financial values

1. Resident booking GMV

2. PetSaathi service revenue

3. Society partnership-fee revenue

4. Net contribution after direct costs

Do not describe total resident booking value as PetSaathi revenue.

Contribution formula

Society contribution =

Society monthly fee

+

PetSaathi margin from resident services

− Resident discounts funded by PetSaathi

− Sitter payments

− Payment-provider costs

− Society coordinator allocation

− Support cost

− Refund/recovery cost

− Event allocation

Example pilot economics

Resident service GMV: ₹22,000

PetSaathi service contribution: ₹6,500

Direct pilot support cost: ₹5,000

Discount subsidy: ₹1,200

Pilot contribution before fee: ₹300

Recommended paid plan:

Basic Society Partner:

₹9,999/month + applicable taxes

Expected post-conversion contribution:

Monthly fee: ₹9,999

Service contribution: ₹6,500

Estimated direct costs: ₹7,500

Estimated society contribution: ₹8,999

These values are examples only. Use real pilot data in every proposal.

Where PetSaathi is required to issue a tax invoice for a taxable service, CBIC’s invoice rules generally require a service invoice within 30 days of the supply of service. Final GST registration, classification and invoicing treatment should be confirmed with a chartered accountant.

4. Conversion-readiness decision model

Use these outcomes instead of a simple yes/no decision.

BASIC_PARTNER_READY

Use when:

30+ qualified resident leads

15+ completed paid bookings

5+ repeat customers

4.5+ rating with sufficient reviews

Operations stable

Society wants continued support

Moderate reporting and support needs

Recommended package:

Basic Society Partner

₹5,000–₹15,000/month

PREMIUM_DESK_READY

Use when:

50+ active or qualified pet households

Strong recurring walking demand

Multiple walking windows

Complex gates or towers

40–50+ completed monthly services

Coordinator demand exists

Events and local partnerships are valuable

Recommended package:

Premium Pet-Care Desk

₹15,000–₹50,000/month

EVENT_MODEL_READY

Use when:

Society engagement is high

Recurring-care bookings remain limited

Camps or workshops receive stronger demand

The society does not need ongoing management

Recommended package:

Event/Camp Model

Event-specific pricing

EXTEND_PILOT

Use when:

Resident demand appears promising

Sample is too small

Repeat customers have not matured

One fixable operational issue remains

No critical safety blocker exists

Recommended extension:

14–30 additional days

with defined targets

Do not extend free pilots indefinitely.

PAUSE_AND_FIX

Use when:

Access failures are frequent

Report Cards are missing

Sitter supply is weak

Support workload is excessive

Payment/refund workflow is unstable

DO_NOT_CONVERT

Use when:

No meaningful resident demand

No usable local sitter supply

Critical incidents remain unresolved

Society obstructs safety controls

Society expects unlawful pet-control practices

Data-access expectations are unacceptable

Economics are structurally negative

Day 46 — Resolve commercial objections

“Why should the society pay when residents already pay?”

Response:

“Resident charges pay for individual care services. The society retainer covers programme management, approved-access coordination, resident support, reporting, complaint escalation and society-level operations.”

“Can we continue the pilot for free?”

Response:

“The no-fee period was designed to validate demand. Continued support, reporting and access management require a defined paid scope. We can also offer a smaller Basic Lite plan or time-limited extension tied to specific targets.”

“Can you reduce the price?”

Consider:

Lower support hours

Fewer resident campaigns

Quarterly rather than monthly event

Basic reporting

Lower active-household cap

Do not reduce price without reducing scope.

11. Common conversion mistakes

Mistake 1 — Converting based on registrations

Correction: Require completed paid services and repeat behaviour.

Mistake 2 — Calling repeat requests repeat customers

Correction: Track second paid and completed services.

Mistake 3 — Presenting ratings without review count

Correction: Always show rating, sample size and response rate.

Mistake 4 — Hiding operational problems

Correction: Show issues, corrective actions and current status.

Mistake 5 — Pricing without cost analysis

Correction: Calculate coordinator, support, event and reporting costs.

Mistake 6 — Reducing price but keeping all features

Correction: Reduce scope when reducing price.

Mistake 7 — Continuing free support indefinitely

Correction: Set an extension deadline and measurable targets.

Mistake 8 — Ambiguous billing

Correction: Separate society-retainer invoices from resident service transactions.

Mistake 9 — Sending private resident details in the conversion report

Correction: Use aggregated data and anonymised feedback.

Mistake 10 — Publishing inflated pilot claims

Correction: Every conversion claim must match the underlying records and remain supportable.

Final conversion rule

A society should pay because PetSaathi has demonstrated ongoing operational value—not merely because the free-pilot period has ended.

The strongest conversion proof is:

Residents registered

+

Residents paid

+

Services completed reliably

+

Customers booked again

+

Society operations improved

+

Economics support continued delivery

Simple explanation for professor

“During Weeks 6 to 8, PetSaathi will convert successful free society pilots into paid partnerships.

In Week 6, we will audit each society’s results. We will examine qualified resident leads, completed paid bookings, second bookings, ratings, punctuality, Report Card delivery, complaints, sitter capacity and financial performance.

A society should normally have at least 30 qualified pet-parent leads, 15 completed paid services, five repeat customers, a 4.5 or higher rating and positive society feedback. It should also have no unresolved critical incident and a workable sitter-access process.

In Week 7, PetSaathi will present the pilot results to the RWA or facility team. We will recommend the correct package based on the society’s actual demand and operational requirements.

For example, a society with 42 resident registrations, 28 paid bookings, a 4.7 rating and seven completed repeat services may be recommended the Basic Society Partner plan at ₹9,999 per month plus applicable taxes.

The monthly fee will cover society-level coordination, support, reporting and sitter-access management. Residents will continue paying separately for the care services they book.

In Week 8, the society and PetSaathi will sign the paid agreement, configure billing, activate the selected package and notify residents. The team will then establish a baseline for the first paid month and schedule regular performance reviews.

The final goal is not simply to end the free pilot. It is to prove that PetSaathi provides enough measurable value for the society to continue paying for the partnership.”

A monthly compliance review covering DPDP, consumer disclosures, GST invoicing and WhatsApp messaging would be prudent before each renewal cycle.

PetSaathi Phase 7 — Paid Conversion and City-Specific Strategy 🏢🐾

Executive decision

The overall strategy is sound, with two important corrections:

The ₹9,999 Basic Society Partner package should be a recommended standard conversion offer—not an automatic price for every society.

Claims such as “Bengaluru users adopt digitally faster” or “Surat users are slower digitally” should be treated as pilot hypotheses, not established facts. India had roughly one billion internet subscribers by mid-2025, while the Internet in India 2025 study reported approximately 958 million active internet users. All six cities can therefore support a mobile-first PWA and digital payments; the real difference is likely to be trust, acquisition channel, society access and support expectations.

Part A — Weeks 6–8: Convert Pilots into Paid Partnerships

Core goal

Convert successful no-fee pilots into recurring society partnerships without extending free operational support indefinitely.

The conversion lifecycle should be:

Pilot completed

↓

Results verified

↓

Society health score calculated

↓

Committee review meeting

↓

Recommended package presented

↓

Commercial negotiation

↓

Paid agreement signed

↓

Invoice/payment completed

↓

Paid partnership activated

A society should not convert merely because the committee says it liked the pilot. It must show evidence of:

Resident demand

Paid usage

Repeat behaviour

Reliable sitter operations

Positive customer experience

Manageable support workload

Acceptable economics

A society with 40 leads and poor service quality should not convert. A smaller society with 22 leads, 18 completed bookings and strong repeat behaviour may still deserve conversion.

Part B — City-Specific Phase 7 Strategy

Important market interpretation

The listed neighbourhoods are sensible candidate micro-markets, but premium property activity does not automatically prove pet-parent demand.

Every city still requires:

Society research

+ resident opt-in survey

+ sitter supply validation

+ security feasibility

+ paid trial demand

Current property and office-market data support targeting technology, employment and premium residential corridors in Bengaluru, Pune, Mumbai, Gurugram and Ahmedabad. They do not provide reliable society-level pet ownership figures.

1. Bengaluru

Bengaluru’s current office-market activity remains strongly concentrated around Outer Ring Road and Peripheral East, supported by technology, GCC and IT-BPM demand. That supports the working-professional positioning around Whitefield, Bellandur and nearby eastern corridors.

Recommended micro-clusters

Cluster A — Whitefield

Hero service: Daily dog walkingAudience: Working professionals in large gated communitiesBest message:

Verified society-access dog walkers with live service status and Pet Report Cards.

Cluster B — Bellandur and Sarjapur Road

Sarjapur Road has continued to develop as a major residential micro-market, with substantial housing supply across mid-market and premium segments.

Hero service: Walking plus travel-time sittingRisk: Traffic can make large service radiuses unreliable.

Cluster C — HSR Layout

Hero service: Pet-parent community and premium sittingChannel: Resident groups, local vets and dog parks

Hero service: Value-oriented five- and ten-walk plansPositioning: Reliable office-hour support rather than luxury concierge

Operating rules

Use a 2–4 km effective sitter radius.

Do not treat Whitefield, HSR, Bellandur and Electronic City as one operational cluster.

Show real-time service status.

Make mobile booking and payment extremely smooth.

Offer same-sitter repeat plans quickly.

Basic Partner: ₹9,999–₹15,000

Premium Desk for large townships: ₹25,000+

Trial-to-pack conversion should be central

Pune continues to show strong office activity, with eastern and western business districts driving demand. Kharadi, Hinjewadi and Viman Nagar are major contributors to the city’s office inventory, supporting a working-professional and recurring-service strategy.

Position packages around value and continuity, not only discounts.

Basic Partner at ₹7,999–₹9,999 for early conversion

₹9,999–₹15,000 for larger multi-tower societies

Premium Desk only after recurring volume is demonstrated

Final paid-conversion wording

Your society pilot has demonstrated genuine resident demand and stable operations.

During the pilot, 42 residents registered, 28 paid bookings were created, the service achieved a 4.7 average rating and 12 residents requested repeat care.

We recommend continuing with the PetSaathi Basic Society Partner plan at ₹9,999 per month plus applicable taxes for an initial three-month term.

The plan includes a society-specific resident portal, partner benefits, priority eligible-sitter matching, an active sitter-access roster, dedicated WhatsApp support, a named operations owner and monthly aggregate reporting.

Residents will continue to pay separately for the care services they book.

Final operating principle

Convert a society because it has demonstrated recurring demand, service reliability and sustainable economics—not merely because the pilot period has ended.

Simple explanation for professor

“During Weeks 6 to 8, PetSaathi will analyse every society pilot and decide whether it should become a paid partnership.

A society will be considered ready when it has enough resident leads, paid bookings, repeat customers, positive ratings, stable sitter access and manageable complaints. We will also check punctuality, Report Card delivery, security-entry performance and financial sustainability.

PetSaathi will prepare a pilot-results report and present it to the society committee. For example, if a society has 42 registrations, 28 paid bookings, a 4.7 rating and 12 repeat requests, we can recommend the Basic Society Partner plan at ₹9,999 per month.

The society fee will pay for programme management, reporting, support, sitter-access coordination and the resident portal. Residents will continue paying separately for dog walking, pet sitting and other services.

The city strategy will also differ. Bengaluru and Pune should focus on smooth digital booking and recurring walking plans. Mumbai must remain highly local because travel is difficult. Gurugram should emphasise verification and premium support. Ahmedabad should use family trust and local veterinary partnerships. Surat should begin with events, referrals and assisted WhatsApp booking.

PetSaathi should first prove the model in one city and a few nearby areas before expanding to another city.”

PetSaathi Society Unit Economics — Corrected End-to-End Model 📊🐾

Executive assessment

Your model is directionally correct, but four financial definitions must be fixed:

Resident booking value is GMV, not automatically PetSaathi revenue.

Track orders, service units and paying households separately.

The estimate of 40–70 pet families in 500 flats is a planning hypothesis, not a confirmed fact.

₹1 lakh from only 50–150 monthly services requires a high-value mix of sitting, boarding or packages; it is unlikely from ordinary walking alone.

Current marketplace listings show substantial pricing variation. ThePetNest states that dog walks in India can range around ₹200–₹600, while individual Ahmedabad listings currently show approximately ₹140–₹210 per walk. Its boarding pages show starting prices from roughly ₹500 nationally and around ₹1,000 per night in Ahmedabad, while home sitting may begin near ₹100 per hour. These should be treated as market observations, not guaranteed city averages.

1. Start with the correct society funnel

For a 500-flat society, use this as an initial hypothesis:

500 total flats

↓

425–475 occupied flats

↓

40–70 pet-owning households

↓

20–30 interested leads

↓

10–20 first-time paying households

↓

5–10 repeat households

↓

50–150 monthly service units

The 40–70 pet-family estimate represents 8–14% of total flats. It is reasonable as an internal planning range, but it must be validated using:

Resident survey

Society pet-parent group

Facility-manager estimate

Veterinary or pet-shop referrals

Previous society events

Actual PetSaathi registrations

Do not present it to investors or society committees as a verified number until the survey confirms it.

2. Do not use “booking” as one universal unit

The word booking becomes misleading because these are economically different:

One 30-minute dog walk

One 2-hour pet-sitting visit

One five-night boarding reservation

One ten-walk package purchase

Use four separate metrics:

### Table 111

| Metric | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Orders | Customer transactions or booking records |
| Service units | Walks, sitting visits or boarding nights delivered |
| Paying households | Unique resident customers |
| Package credits | Walks or visits purchased but not yet consumed |

Example

A resident purchasing a ten-walk package creates:

1 order

10 service credits

10 future service units

1 paying household

Without this separation, revenue, workload and repeat usage become inaccurate.

3. Correct financial terminology

Resident GMV

The total value paid by residents:

Resident GMV =

Dog-walking payments

+ sitting payments

+ boarding payments

+ other resident service payments

Sitter or partner payout

The amount paid to:

Dog walkers

Pet sitters

Boarding hosts

Groomers

Veterinary or training partners

Platform service margin

An internal planning formula:

Platform service margin =

Resident GMV − sitter/partner payouts

Net service contribution

A more useful metric:

Net service contribution =

Platform service margin

− payment-processing cost

− funded discounts

− refunds

− booking-specific support cost

− incident/recovery reserve

Total society contribution

Total society contribution =

Net service contribution

+ society subscription

+ event coordination contribution

− society coordinator cost

− society marketing cost

− onboarding allocation

− other society-specific costs

This is the number that shows whether one society is economically useful.

4. Payment-processing cost

Razorpay’s standard pricing currently states 2% plus GST per successful transaction, which creates an effective cost of approximately 2.36% when 18% GST is applied to the 2% gateway fee.

Therefore:

### Table 112

| Resident GMV | Approximate gateway cost |
| --- | --- |
| ₹25,000 | ₹590 |
| ₹50,000 | ₹1,180 |
| ₹1,00,000 | ₹2,360 |

Payment-gateway cost should be deducted before calling the service margin contribution.

5. Corrected 500-flat society model

The following scenarios use an illustrative 30% platform service margin. This is not an industry standard; replace it with PetSaathi’s actual sitter-payout rules.

Scenario A — Conservative walking-heavy society

Assumptions

Pet families: 40

Interested leads: 20

Paying households: 10

Monthly service units: 60

Service mix:

This society may be operationally useful because of density, but resident bookings alone do not produce strong profit yet.

Assumptions

Monthly service units: 150

Service mix:

### Table 117

| Boarding nights | 15 | ₹1,200 | ₹18,000 |
| Resident GMV |  |  | ₹63,000 |

Economics:

### Table 118

| Item | Amount |

| Society subscription | +₹15,000 |
| Event contribution | +₹15,000 |
| Contribution before coordinator/overhead | ₹47,413 |

Interpretation

A society can reach strong economics when it combines:

High-frequency walks

Higher-value sitting
