Phase 7 definition of done

Phase 7 is complete only when:

Sales system

Packages are approved.

Pricing is consistent.

CRM stages work.

Outreach is personalised and permission-based.

Pitch and proposal claims are supportable.

Product system

Society pages work.

Resident verification works.

Society offers work.

Society booking and pricing snapshots work.

Public/private data separation works.

Operations

Gate protocols are active.

Local sitter pools have backup capacity.

Booking caps prevent overload.

Support and incident escalation work.

Events have qualified partners.

Reporting and economics

Pilot reports are generated.

GMV and PetSaathi revenue are not confused.

Society subscriptions and booking contributions are separated.

Complaints and incidents are measurable.

At least one society demonstrates positive or improving contribution.

Partnership proof

Five to ten societies have signed pilots.

Three to five produce real paid activity.

Residents complete services and submit reviews.

Repeat demand exists.

At least one society agrees to continue under a paid package.

Final operating principle

Phase 7 succeeds when PetSaathi can repeatedly acquire, launch, operate, measure and convert apartment societies without compromising service quality, privacy or lawful pet ownership.

The win is not ten society logos.

It is:

Active residents

+ reliable local sitters

+ successful gate entry

+ completed paid services

+ repeat demand

+ measurable reporting

+ paid society renewals

Phase 7 Society Partnership Report

End-to-End Strategy, Operating Model and Go/No-Go Framework

Executive decision

Phase 7 should be approved—with several important modifications.

The society-first strategy is stronger than ordinary local advertising because a residential society can become all of the following simultaneously:

A concentrated customer-acquisition channel

A controlled operating zone

A trust and verification layer

A recurring-booking cluster

A sitter-supply territory

A referral network

A service-quality data unit

The original concept already identifies society partnerships, verified caregivers, GPS tracking, report cards and emergency support as core differentiators.

However, Phase 7 must not be measured only through total leads, bookings and ratings. It must prove that multiple individual societies can produce repeatable, contribution-positive demand without creating security, safety or resident-conflict problems.

Final Phase 7 verdict

Build a society operating system, not an RWA advertising campaign.

1. Why society-first growth can work

India’s urban pet-care demand is being supported by rising pet ownership, pet humanisation, nuclear households and increased spending on pet health and services. Purina cites reports estimating that household pets in India increased from approximately 26 million in 2019 to 32 million in 2024, while IBEF identifies urbanisation, higher spending and changing attitudes toward companion animals as major industry drivers.

The society model adds something that city-wide advertising cannot provide: geographic density.

When five customers live in one building cluster:

A sitter can complete more bookings with less travel.

A backup caregiver can arrive quickly.

Residents can refer other residents.

Security personnel recognise approved caregivers.

Service quality becomes visible through community reputation.

Customer acquisition can be shared across many households.

Repeat walks can be organised into efficient schedules.

This is why the society itself should be treated as a micro-market.

2. Phase 7 autopsy: what the original framework gets right

Strong elements

### Table 145

| Element | Assessment |
| --- | --- |
| Society-specific landing pages | Strong acquisition and attribution mechanism |
| Verified local sitter pools | Essential for trust and service reliability |
| Resident introductory offers | Useful when controlled |
| Gate and security protocol | Mandatory operational infrastructure |
| Pet-care camps | Strong resident activation mechanism |
| Monthly society reports | Useful for RWA confidence and renewal |
| Thirty-day pilots | Long enough to test first bookings and early repeats |
| Paid society partnerships | Valuable, but not realistic for every society |
| Repeat customers | More important than total registrations |
| Lower CAC than advertisements | Correct ultimate win condition |

The strategic direction is correct. The weakness is mainly in definitions, measurement discipline and commercial structure.

3. Hidden weaknesses exposed

3.1 “Free society pilot” is dangerously ambiguous

A free pilot should mean:

No setup or partnership fee for the RWA.

It should not mean:

Free dog walking, sitting or boarding for every resident for 30 days.

Unlimited free services would test demand for free care—not willingness to pay.

Correct pilot structure

RWA pays no partnership fee during the pilot.

Residents pay for services.

New residents may receive one limited introductory discount.

The platform places a fixed ceiling on promotional expenditure.

Sitters still receive proper compensation.

Refunds and discounts are recorded separately.

Conversion is measured using full-price or near-full-price bookings.

Recommended introductory offer:

₹100–₹200 first-booking discount, or

One discounted trial walk, or

Free meet-and-greet followed by paid service

Do not provide unlimited free pet care.

3.2 Total results can hide weak societies

Suppose Phase 7 reports:

250 leads

120 bookings

35 repeat customers

That can appear successful even when one society generated 80% of the results and every other society failed.

Therefore, Phase 7 must measure:

Total results

Median results per society

Best society

Worst society

Mature-society results

Concentration of bookings

Contribution margin by society

A society model is repeatable only when at least two or three societies perform independently.

3.3 “Pet parent lead” requires a strict definition

A person who scans a QR code is not necessarily a qualified lead.

Recommended lead stages

### Table 146

| Stage | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Reach | Resident saw an announcement, poster or event |
| Inquiry | Resident opened the page or contacted the team |
| Registered lead | Resident submitted contact details with consent |
| Qualified pet lead | Resident has a pet and selected a required service |
| Booking-ready lead | Resident selected an expected date or frequency |
| Paying customer | Completed at least one paid booking |
| Repeat customer | Completed another paid booking within the defined period |

Your Phase 7 target of 200+ leads should mean qualified pet-parent leads, not page visits or event attendees.

3.4 RWA subscriptions may be harder than expected

Most RWAs will ask:

“Why should the society pay for a service used only by some residents?”

A society subscription therefore needs to provide society-level value—not merely individual pet-care bookings.

Three commercial models

### Table 147

| Model | Who pays? | Recommended use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Resident-paid marketplace | Residents | Default model |
| Sponsored community programme | Pet brand, veterinary partner or local sponsor | Events and initial acquisition |
| Paid society care plan | RWA, facility manager or developer | Premium communities needing dedicated coverage |

A paid society plan must include

Dedicated local caregiver availability

Defined backup response

Monthly pet-care camp or clinic

Gate-approved caregiver roster

Resident support hotline

Incident escalation

Aggregate monthly report

Preferential resident pricing

Community pet-awareness sessions

Optional emergency-care coordination

Do not expect societies to pay merely for being listed as partners.

3.5 Events can produce vanity metrics

A pet camp may attract:

Children

Non-pet-owning residents

People looking for free veterinary advice

Residents collecting giveaways

Existing customers who would have booked anyway

Therefore, an event should be measured as a conversion funnel:

An event is successful only if it produces paying, retained customers at an acceptable acquisition cost.

3.6 Rating alone is insufficient

An average rating of 4.8 from five reviews is much weaker evidence than a rating of 4.65 from 100 verified bookings.

Track:

Average rating

Number of ratings

Rating participation rate

Percentage of five-star ratings

Percentage below four stars

Complaint rate

Refund rate

Serious incident count

Only reviews attached to completed bookings should count.

3.7 Phase 8 is too broad

Adding grooming, veterinary care, training and pet taxi simultaneously would recreate the original problem of excessive scope.

Phase 8 should add one adjacent service at a time, selected using Phase 7 customer-request data.

A better order is:

Grooming-at-home or training

Veterinary booking or teleconsultation through licensed partners

Pet taxi

Other specialist services

The chosen service should first launch in two or three proven societies—not throughout the city.

4. Approval decision on the five rules

### Table 148

| Rule | Decision | Required modification |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Society-specific landing pages | Approve | One unique page and attribution code per society |
| 30-day free society pilot | Approve conditionally | Free for the RWA; resident services remain paid |
| Mandatory gate/security protocol | Approve—hard requirement | No society launches without a signed access SOP |
| Monthly society report | Approve | Use aggregate, privacy-safe reporting |
| Event/camp acquisition | Approve as an experiment | Test against non-event societies and measure CAC |

Final rule recommendation

Approve all five, but rewrite the pilot rule as follows:

“Thirty-day zero-setup-fee society pilot with paid resident bookings, limited introductory incentives and predefined conversion criteria.”

5. Ideal target society profile

Do not begin with every society in the city.

Best initial society type

The best Phase 7 target is likely to be:

500–1,500 occupied units

Gated access and organised security

Active managing committee or professional facility manager

Concentration of working professionals and families

Existing pet-owning community

Clubhouse or event area

Reasonable acceptance of pets

Multiple nearby societies within a 3–5 km service radius

No severe unresolved pet-related conflict

Easy access to a veterinary clinic

Ability to send official resident communications

This is an operating hypothesis. Phase 7 data should confirm or reject it.

Society scoring model

Score each potential society out of 100.

### Table 149

| Factor | Weight |
| --- | --- |
| Number of occupied households | 15 |
| Estimated pet-parent density | 20 |
| Resident spending capacity | 10 |
| RWA responsiveness | 10 |
| Gate/access feasibility | 10 |
| Existing pet community | 10 |
| Event-space availability | 5 |
| Nearby sitter supply | 10 |
| Nearby veterinary support | 5 |
| Nearby society cluster | 5 |

Priority levels

75–100: Priority A

60–74: Priority B

Below 60: Do not prioritise during Phase 7

6. Society acquisition engine

The society acquisition system has two separate funnels:

Funnel A: acquiring the society

Funnel B: acquiring residents inside the society

These funnels should not be mixed.

Recommended society acquisition channels

1. Resident pet champion

A resident introduces the company to the managing committee.

This is likely to be the strongest route because it transfers existing trust.

Offer the champion:

Referral credits

Early access

Free meet-and-greet

Recognition as a community pet coordinator

No authority over complaints or sitter approval

2. Veterinary and grooming partners

Local veterinary clinics and grooming providers often already know clusters of nearby pet owners.

Use them for:

Warm introductions

Camp participation

Emergency support

Joint educational sessions

Resident offers

3. Facility-management companies

Large societies may be operated by professional facility-management teams rather than volunteer committees.

Your pitch should focus on:

Approved vendor roster

Access control

Complaint handling

Safety procedures

Reporting

Reduced administrative effort

4. Society management platforms

Common society-management platforms support visitor management, pre-approved daily help, attendance records, resident communication and gate processes. This shows that a digitally controlled caregiver-entry workflow is compatible with existing community operations, although any actual integration would require the relevant society and platform’s approval.

5. Cold RWA outreach

Cold email and messaging should be used primarily to create meetings—not to close partnerships.

Cold outreach should contain:

One clear community benefit

One-page safety framework

Pilot structure

No-obligation 20-minute meeting

Local caregiver availability

Existing resident request, where applicable

7. The correct RWA value proposition

Do not pitch:

“We need access to sell pet services to your residents.”

Pitch:

“We provide a controlled, verified system for residents who already need pet care, with approved caregivers, gate procedures, service accountability and a single escalation channel.”

What the RWA actually cares about

Who enters the society?

Has the person been verified?

Who is accountable if something happens?

Will dogs be leashed?

Who cleans pet waste?

Will residents complain?

Will commercial solicitation become uncontrolled?

Is the society legally exposed?

Can access be suspended immediately?

Will resident data be misused?

Will the provider create conflict between pet and non-pet residents?

The sales presentation must answer these before discussing discounts or revenue.

8. RWA objection-response framework

### Table 150

| RWA objection | Correct response |
| --- | --- |
| “We do not want unknown workers entering.” | Use an approved caregiver roster, ID verification, pre-authorised entry and attendance logs |
| “What if a dog bites someone?” | Behaviour screening, leash rules, handler matching, incident SOP and emergency escalation |
| “Who will clean pet waste?” | Walkers carry disposal bags and must document compliance |
| “The society may become liable.” | Define responsibility contractually; obtain legal review and appropriate insurance |
| “Residents will complain about noise.” | Service-hour rules, designated routes and complaint escalation |
| “We do not permit advertising.” | Use only committee-approved messages and events |
| “Do not share resident data.” | Collect resident consent directly and provide only anonymised aggregate reports |
| “We already have local dog walkers.” | Offer verification, backup coverage, tracking and accountability rather than forced replacement |
| “We do not want exclusivity.” | Do not require exclusivity during the pilot |
| “Why should the RWA pay?” | Begin with the resident-paid model; offer a paid plan only for additional society-level services |

AWBI guidance states that societies should not impose blanket bans on pets or prevent pets from using lifts, while also advising pet owners and caregivers to maintain cleanliness, vaccinations and leashing in public areas. The more recent AWBI SOP also emphasises leashing and basic command training. Your RWA approach should therefore be cooperative and safety-focused rather than confrontational.

9. Society-specific landing page

Each society should receive its own URL:

petsaathi.in/society/society-name

Required page sections

Society identity

Society name

Target area

Partnership or pilot status

Society-specific referral code

Pilot validity period

Available services

Dog walking

In-home pet visits

Meet-and-greet

Emergency coordination

Later services only when approved

Local caregiver pool

Display:

First name

Photograph

Verification level

Pet-handling experience

Service availability

Supported dog sizes

Languages

Rating

Number of completed verified bookings

Do not publicly reveal:

Full personal address

Government ID

Personal phone number

Police documents

Unmasked emergency contacts

Resident offer

Exact discount

Eligibility

Expiration

Cancellation conditions

Whether the offer is platform-funded or partner-funded

Trust and safety

Identity and background-check status

Leash policy

Waste-cleanup policy

Check-in procedure

GPS/report-card information

Complaint contact

Emergency protocol

Booking flow

Society and tower

Pet profile

Behaviour disclosures

Requested service

Date and time

Care instructions

Emergency contact

Veterinary details

Payment

Consent

Attribution

Every booking must store:

Society ID

Acquisition channel

Campaign or event ID

Resident referral

First-contact date

First-booking date

Without attribution, society CAC cannot be calculated accurately.

10. Society-specific sitter pool

A local sitter pool should not merely be a collection of available profiles.

It must be a coverage system.

Recommended starter configuration

For each active society or tightly grouped society cluster:

2–4 primary caregivers

At least one backup caregiver

One operations escalation contact

One veterinary partner

Defined morning and evening coverage

Capability mapping by dog size and behaviour

Sitter capability matrix

### Table 151

| Capability | Example levels |
| --- | --- |
| Dog size | Small / medium / large |
| Behaviour | Calm / anxious / reactive |
| Service | Walking / sitting / medication support |
| Availability | Morning / afternoon / evening |
| Experience | Beginner / trained / advanced |
| Access | Society-approved / pending |
| Emergency training | Completed / pending |
| Languages | English / Hindi / local language |

Supply metrics

Track:

Available sitter hours

Booked sitter hours

Peak-period utilisation

Backup fill rate

Travel time between bookings

Sitter cancellation rate

Sitter earnings

Sitter retention

A starting planning heuristic is to maintain approximately 1.5 times the required sitter capacity during peak pilot periods. This should be recalibrated using actual demand.

11. Mandatory gate and security protocol

This is not merely a guard instruction sheet. It is a complete chain of authorisation.

Gate protocol flow

Before the first booking

RWA approves the pilot.

Approved caregiver roster is shared securely.

Each caregiver receives a society-specific ID.

Guards receive a briefing.

Resident entry-authorisation method is confirmed.

Escalation phone numbers are displayed at the gate desk.

Before each visit

Booking is confirmed.

Resident receives sitter details.

Resident pre-approves entry.

Caregiver receives booking ID or QR code.

The system confirms time window and permitted tower.

At entry

Caregiver presents official ID.

Guard matches caregiver against the approved roster.

Resident confirms entry through OTP, call or society app.

Entry time is logged.

Any mismatch is escalated—not manually bypassed.

During service

Caregiver remains within the permitted service area.

No unrelated guest accompanies the caregiver.

Dog remains leashed outside the residence.

Waste bags are carried.

No dog is handed to another person without authorisation.

No unapproved pet interaction is permitted.

No photographs of unrelated residents or flats are taken.

At exit

Service completion is recorded.

Dog is returned to the authorised person or residence.

Exit is logged.

Any incident is documented before the caregiver leaves.

Report card is sent to the pet parent.

Gate KPIs

Entry success rate

Average gate waiting time

Entry-denial rate

Number of manual overrides

Unauthorised-entry incidents

Guard escalations

Caregiver identification failures

Recommended target

Gate success: 98%+

Unauthorised entry: Zero

Unresolved access incidents: Zero

Manual override rate: Below 5%

12. Pet-care camp model

Purpose

The camp is not merely a public-relations event.

It should accomplish four things:

Acquire qualified pet-parent leads

Build trust with non-pet residents and the RWA

Create pet profiles and first bookings

Identify common resident needs

Recommended camp stations

Registration desk

Resident consent

Pet profile

Requested service

Expected service frequency

Preferred booking date

Veterinary desk

Operated only by appropriately qualified veterinary professionals.

Potential activities:

General wellness discussion

Vaccination-record review

Nutrition awareness

Parasite-control education

Emergency-preparedness guidance

Clinical services and medical claims should remain under the responsibility of the licensed veterinary provider.

Behaviour and safety desk

Leash demonstration

Basic walking etiquette

Pet anxiety discussion

Safe introductions

Children-and-pet awareness

Caregiver introduction zone

Meet local caregivers

Explain verification levels

Demonstrate report cards

Demonstrate tracking and check-in

Booking desk

The resident should be able to:

Complete a pet profile

Select a caregiver

Book a meet-and-greet

Schedule the first paid service

Purchase a recurring plan

Event measurement

### Table 152

| Metric | Formula |
| --- | --- |
| Qualified-lead rate | Qualified pet leads ÷ attendees |
| Booking conversion | First paid customers ÷ qualified pet leads |
| Event CAC | Direct event cost ÷ first paid customers |
| Repeat rate | Repeat customers ÷ eligible first customers |
| Event revenue | Revenue attributable to event-acquired customers |
| Event payback | Contribution generated ÷ event cost |

A recommended pilot target is:

25+ qualified leads per meaningful event

20%+ qualified-lead-to-paid conversion within 14 days

30%+ 30-day repeat among eligible customers

These are internal starting targets, not universal market benchmarks.

13. Thirty-day society pilot design

Pre-pilot: Days −7 to 0

Sign pilot memorandum

Finalise gate SOP

Verify caregiver pool

Create society page

Install attribution

Train guards

Prepare resident communications

Schedule camp

Set complaint and emergency contacts

Establish baseline metrics

Week 1: Awareness and onboarding

Official society announcement

Resident champion introduction

QR posters in approved areas

Pet-parent registrations

Free meet-and-greets

First paid bookings

Week 2: Activation

Conduct pet-care camp

Introduce local caregivers

Convert qualified residents

Begin recurring-plan promotion

Review first complaints and gate failures

Week 3: Retention

Offer recurring walking plans

Prioritise the same caregiver

Contact first-time customers

Resolve low ratings

Conduct service-quality audits

Week 4: Conversion and reporting

Calculate customer repeat rate

Calculate society CAC

Review contribution margin

Present anonymised RWA report

Submit paid partnership proposal

Decide renew, extend or stop

14. Monthly society report

The report to the RWA should contain aggregate operational information only.

Suitable information

Number of active resident customers

Number of completed bookings

Number of approved caregivers

On-time performance

Complaint count

Gate incidents

Safety incidents

Event participation

Resident rating

General improvement actions

Information that should not be disclosed

Individual customer booking history

Flat-specific pet behaviour

Customer phone numbers

Medical records

Payment details

Private complaints

Exact caregiver identity records

Personal GPS routes beyond what is operationally necessary

The Digital Personal Data Protection Act requires consent-based processing to be specific, informed and limited to the stated purpose, and gives individuals rights concerning withdrawal, correction and erasure. The final 2025 Rules use phased commencement dates, but Phase 7 should be designed toward the full consent, minimisation, security and deletion standard now.

15. Complaint and incident system

Complaint classes

### Table 153

| Severity | Example | Response |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P0 — Critical | Escape, bite, serious injury, missing pet | Immediate live escalation |
| P1 — High | Unauthorised access, sitter unreachable, pet not returned on time | Immediate operational intervention |
| P2 — Medium | Major delay, incorrect care instructions, unacceptable behaviour | Same-day resolution |
| P3 — Standard | Billing, report-card issue, minor dissatisfaction | Resolve through normal support |

Required incident record

Booking ID

Society

Pet

Caregiver

Location

Time

Description

Photographs or evidence

Witnesses

Immediate action

Veterinary involvement

Customer communication

Root cause

Corrective action

Final resolution

Phase 7 safety gate

Any preventable serious safety incident should pause expansion until a documented root-cause review and corrective action are completed.

16. Legal and compliance layer

Animal and society rules

AWBI guidance advises leashing, vaccination and waste cleanup, and provides guidance concerning pet-owning residents and RWAs. The Animal Birth Control Rules, 2023 separately assign responsibilities concerning community-animal feeding locations and conflict resolution. Do not mix the commercial pet-care pilot with community-dog management unless the agreement explicitly includes that scope.

Consumer protection

A digital marketplace should provide:

Clear service descriptions

Transparent pricing

Cancellation terms

Refund terms

Caregiver information

Customer support

Grievance contact

Complaint tracking

No misleading “fully insured” or “government certified” claims

The Consumer Protection E-Commerce Rules require e-commerce entities to maintain an adequate grievance-redressal mechanism and display the responsible officer’s contact information.

Data protection

Collect only what is necessary:

Resident identity and contact

Society and access information

Pet profile

Care instructions

Veterinary and emergency details

Booking information

Use separate consent for:

Booking fulfilment

Marketing messages

Society reporting

Photos and testimonials

Sharing information with caregivers

Sharing information with veterinary partners

GST and marketplace structure

The tax treatment depends on whether the business acts as:

Direct service provider

Managed marketplace

Electronic-commerce operator

Agent collecting on behalf of sitters

Principal purchasing and reselling services

CBIC defines an electronic-commerce operator as a person operating or managing a digital facility for electronic commerce, and its guidance indicates that electronic-commerce operators can face registration and tax-collection obligations. The final model should be reviewed by a qualified chartered accountant before scale.

Required agreements

RWA pilot memorandum

Customer service terms

Sitter or caregiver agreement

Veterinary partner agreement

Data-processing and confidentiality obligations

Incident and emergency authorisation

Refund and cancellation policy

17. Society economics

Contribution formula

Society CAC

Example—not a market benchmark

Assume one society produces:

### Table 154

| Item | Example |
| --- | --- |
| Paid monthly bookings | 100 |
| Average booking value | ₹450 |
| GMV | ₹45,000 |
| Platform take rate | 22% |
| Commission revenue | ₹9,900 |
| Society subscription | ₹5,000 |
| Total gross platform revenue | ₹14,900 |
| Direct monthly society cost | ₹9,000 |
| Contribution | ₹5,900 |

This demonstrates an important reality:

Even 100 bookings may not create strong economics if the average booking value and take rate are low.

Therefore, Phase 7 should also drive:

Recurring walk subscriptions

High booking density

Same-sitter scheduling

Low caregiver travel cost

Low support cost

Higher-value sitting bookings

Resident referrals

Repeatable society definition

A society is commercially repeatable when it produces:

Positive contribution for two consecutive months

Reliable gate operations

Stable sitter coverage

Satisfactory resident ratings

Controlled complaint rates

Demand without continuing heavy discounts

18. Corrected Phase 7 go/no-go criteria

The original targets are directionally good but need stronger operational and economic gates.

Recommended minimum requirements

### Table 155

| Requirement | Phase 8 target |
| --- | --- |
| Live pilot societies | 5–10 |
| Mature pilots with 30 days of data | 5+ |
| Qualified pet-parent leads | 200+ |
| Unique paying customers | 40+ |
| Paid society bookings | 100+ |
| 30-day repeat rate | 30%+ |
| Average rating | 4.6+ |
| Verified review count | 50+ |
| On-time service rate | 95%+ |
| Caregiver cancellation rate | 5% or lower |
| Complaint rate | 3% or lower |
| Serious preventable incidents | 0 |
| Gate-entry success | 98%+ |
| Primary caregiver coverage | Yes |
| Backup caregiver coverage | Yes |
| Event model tested | At least 3 events |
| Event-to-paid conversion measured | Yes |
| Contribution-positive societies | At least 2 |
| Paid/contracted society conversion | At least 30% of mature pilots |
| Society CAC below ad CAC | Demonstrated using comparable test |
| Repeatable monthly demand | Yes |

Critical gates

Phase 8 should not begin unless all four gates are passed.

1. Demand gate

At least 100 paid bookings

At least 40 unique paying customers

At least 30% eligible customer repeat rate

2. Safety gate

Zero unresolved serious incidents

Behaviour and emergency protocols operational

Complaint system tested

3. Operations gate

95%+ on-time service

98%+ successful gate entry

Primary and backup caregiver availability

No dependence on one individual caregiver

4. Economics gate

At least two societies produce positive contribution

Acquisition costs can be calculated accurately

Society CAC is lower than or approaching the paid-ad control

Results are not dependent on unlimited discounts

19. How to compare society CAC with advertisement CAC

The statement “societies generate lower CAC than ads” must be experimentally proven.

Run a controlled comparison

Society group

Two or three society pilots

Same service

Same city cluster

Same introductory offer

All costs included

Advertising group

Meta or Google campaign

Same geographic radius

Same landing-page proposition

Same introductory offer

Same measurement period

Compare

### Table 156

| Metric | Society | Paid ads |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Qualified leads |  |  |
| Paying customers |  |  |
| CAC |  |  |
| 30-day repeat |  |  |
| Bookings per customer |  |  |
| Contribution per customer |  |  |
| Complaint rate |  |  |
| Referral rate |  |  |

The winning channel is not necessarily the channel with the lowest first-booking CAC. It is the channel with the strongest:

over an appropriate cohort period.

| Event model validated | Yes |  |  |
| Society CAC below ad CAC | Yes |  |  |

Final decision

Decision:Move to Phase 8 / Continue Phase 7 / Change area / Improve offer / Stop rollout

Decision rationale:

Conditions before next phase:

Next 30-day action plan:

Responsible owner:

Review date:

21. Phase 8 entry strategy

Passing Phase 7 should not automatically authorise all service categories.

Phase 8 service-selection test

Add a service only when:

At least 25% of active customers request it, or there is equivalent strong evidence.

A reliable and qualified supply partner exists.

Legal and insurance responsibilities are clear.

The projected contribution margin is positive.

The service can use the existing society and gate infrastructure.

It does not weaken walking and sitting quality.

A two-society pilot can be launched safely.

Recommended decision logic

### Table 168

| Customer data shows | Phase 8 service |
| --- | --- |
| Frequent grooming requests | Grooming-at-home |
| Behaviour problems and puppy owners | Training |
| Difficulty visiting clinics | Vet booking or teleconsultation |
| Transport problems | Pet taxi |
| Travel-related demand | Carefully controlled boarding partnership |

Final recommendation

Approve Phase 7 with the following operating definition

A society-first pet-care growth and operations engine with local caregiver pools, resident-paid bookings, controlled gate access, privacy-safe reporting, measurable events and 30-day zero-setup-fee RWA pilots.

The true Phase 7 win condition

A society must reliably produce:

Qualified pet-parent leads

Paid first bookings

Repeat customers

Positive contribution

Reliable caregiver coverage

Smooth gate operations

High resident satisfaction

Manageable complaints

Lower acquisition cost than a comparable advertising campaign

Only then has the society become local marketplace infrastructure rather than a temporary marketing channel.

Final rule decision

### Table 169

| Rule | Final recommendation |
| --- | --- |
| Society-specific landing pages | Approve |
| Thirty-day free society pilot | Modify and approve |
| Mandatory gate/security protocol | Approve as non-negotiable |
| Monthly society report | Approve with privacy controls |
| Event/camp acquisition | Approve as a measured experiment |
